#2886. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2884. Stock market after midterm elections: What S&P 500 history shows

Markets are sweating it out ahead of the midterm elections, but the event historically heralds strong performance for stocks — regardless of whether it's a red or blue wave, split decision, or status quo in Washington.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

The S&P 500 tends to perform well after both midterm and presidential elections. According to a CNBC analysis of S&P 500 returns after elections from 1980 through 2024, the index rose an average of 0.2% after one day and one week, and 1.1% after one month across both presidential and midterm elections.

A year later, the index gained an average of 13.7% after midterms and 13.8% after presidential elections. Returns were positive in 91% of cases. And the S&P 500 was up after all 11 midterms from 1982 through 2022.

Short-term returns were less reliable, particularly after presidential contests, which produced slightly negative average returns after one day and one week.

"There's some anxiousness before these elections," Ryan Detrick, CNBC contributor and chief market strategist at Carson Group, told CNBC. "Once you are through it, it's a big relief and markets tend to do pretty well."

Still, the market usually prefers divided government: one party in charge of the White House and the other holding at least one house of Congress.

Across 10 midterm and five presidential elections that left no single party in charge, the S&P 500 rose an average 15% after one year. One-year returns were positive in 14 out of 15 cases. By comparison, the index gained an average of 11.5% over the year after elections that resulted in single-party control.

"If you have a huge red wave or a big blue wave, markets actually kind of don't like that as much because you have too much spending one way and too much power," Detrick said.

Leaving Democrats in control of Congress with a Republican in the White House — a scenario that may emerge from the elections on Tuesday — was also positive. A CNBC analysis found that after the three midterms (1986, 1990 and 2006) when Democrats controlled both houses and a Republican held the presidency, the S&P 500 averaged a 0.5% decline on the reaction day but was up 3.4% a month later and 10.9% after a year.

Granted, elections may be coincident events to factors that matter far more for market performance.

The S&P 500 plunged 22.1% in the year after the 2000 elections due to the dotcom bust, marking the worst post-election performance since 1980. Conversely, the S&P 500's best post-election year was a 38.3% gain after the 2020 elections, when markets mounted a massive comeback from the COVID-induced selloff.

Investors should bear in mind that Federal Reserve policy and the corporate earnings cycle ultimately matter more than elections, according to Société Générale.

Stocks declined in the aftermath of the 2018 midterms, for instance, when Democrats wrested control of the House during President Donald Trump's first term. More importantly, the Fed tightened monetary policy that year, raising interest rates four times, and the S&P 500 ended the year down 6.2%, though the market was up 11.7% a year after the election.

The big swing factor now is AI and whether declines in tech will continue. As long as the AI wave is still moving and the economy keeps growing, "this bull market is probably still alive and well," Detrick says.

But a Democratic sweep of the House and Senate would almost certainly pressure the AI infrastructure buildout and could lead to more retrenchment.

The bond market is also skittish with yields rising globally and a mini debt crisis in France. And the war with Iran could get worse post-election if President Trump follows through on threats of more strikes or Iran escalates.

"Seasonality isn't destiny, and worries around Iran, AI and France won't mysteriously vanish," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note.

If history is any guide, we could see a Santa rally and S&P 500 gains after the midterms. But this is still an AI-driven market, with geopolitics, the Fed, and bond market very much in charge.

— CNBC's Chris Hayes contributed reporting.

#2882. It’s fun to be a Sixer right now

Typically, he enjoys drinking it when he's not playing, but apparently exceptions can be made.

On Monday night, James went into the crowd going after a loose ball in the third quarter of the Sixers' 126-107 win over the Chicago Bulls. There was a brief stir from the fans. We later found out it was because James had taken a sip from a spectator's cup.

"I crashed into him, spilled some of his wine," James said. "So I wanted to see if it was worth it on my arm, man. How good it was."

Spoiled by California wines, no doubt, but James is profiling well with his new team.

When James' agent Rich Paul did his media blitz during his client's free agency, he mentioned happiness as the biggest factor for the King's decision.

In the fourth quarter, as the Sixers began to pull away, James found himself guarded by the 6-foot-1, 190-pound Tre Jones. LeBron easily bullied Jones into the post. Jones bit on a fake and Leonard Miller came over to help. James pump-faked again, this time getting Miller in the air, absorbing the contact and burying a short fadeaway.

And on the next offensive possession, it was Tyrese Maxey's turn to get into the lane and convert a crazy finish while getting fouled.

Safe to say, this group is having a good time.

"Listen, it's a long marathon," James said, "and if you don't find ways to have fun throughout it — even though you're working, even though you're locked in — it could wear on you. So for me, especially at this point in my career, too, being able to just appreciate the moments, the small moments. These fans are great to us. We try to be great to them as well by playing the game the right way, playing the game hard, and then also just being showmen as well."

James finished the night with 15 points, nine assists and seven rebounds. Maxey with 26 points and 11 assists. Jaylen Brown had 25 points, 11 rebounds and five assists. Joel Embiid, who was on a minutes restriction while managing plantar fasciitis, recorded 15 points, eight rebounds and three assists. The Sixers as a team had 36 assists on 48 makes.

Tough night for the "there's only one ball!" crowd.

And let's not forget about VJ Edgecombe — you know, the guy whose growth was going to be stunted because the Sixers have too many good players. The second-year guard scored a season-high 21 points, his first 20-plus point game on the young season, and dished out five assists.

It was a rewarding scoring night for a guy who's done all the dirty work for the Sixers' starting group. Edgecombe said his lower usage rate isn't his concern, though.

"I don't get antsy or impatient or anything," he said. "We're 7-1 and that's the most important thing. … And like tonight, I was able to get going a little bit, but once you just find that joy out there, just playing basketball … I was just kind of hard on myself early [this season] because I was missing shots I [usually] make and that's not on nobody else but me. And like I say, it's fun when you're winning. I don't want to be a cancer because I ain't getting the shots that I want to get. I'm still learning. I'm in year two."

The chemistry between the starting group is growing. A 7-1 start is a big help, but there seems to be an eagerness for all of them to learn each other. It's something that's been going on since training camp. In fact, a lesson from camp made its way onto the game floor.

James hit Edgecombe with not one but two absurdly accurate bounce passes in transition, but the pair needed to build trust to execute them when it counted.

"There's a couple times in training camp that he kind of slowed up and didn't think I'd get the pass there to him," James said. "And I told him all he has to do is just run, and I'll try to put it on time, on target as much as I can."

After the game, Edgecombe described it as a teaching moment. He was rewarded for that diligence.

Against the Bulls, Edgecombe was ready, flushing down multiple transition opportunities.

"If you're open, he's going to pass it to you," Edgecombe said. "The main thing is just being ready. Tonight, I had to run to go get them."

And this team is still in the fermentation process.

"It's a lot of talent, man," Brown said. "And we like each other and we're just kind of like building that chemistry of how we can get this thing to a well-oiled machine. So I think we're taking steps forward every single day. I think we still got a long way to go to be a really, really solid, great team. But we've been able to win some games. We started out the season 7-1 and you haven't seen our best version yet. So that's a good sign."

#2880. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2878. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2876. Midterm elections could jolt stocks as Democrats look poised to sweep Congress

The stock market was not pricing in a Democratic sweep of Congress heading into Tuesday and therefore the political climate could be a headwind for investors into year-end.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

Short-term moves aside, the typical post-midterm pattern in the months ahead is bullish. But some wonder if the market could buck the positive historical trend this time because the S&P 500 is already up so much this year going into the election.

As far as certain stocks to watch, a big Democratic win on Tuesday night — especially in the Texas governor race — could add fuel to an artificial intelligence stock sell-off on the notion that more data center restrictions would gain momentum nationally. Defense stocks could come under pressure on fears of limits on spending.

Prediction markets calling for a blue sweep are looking to be correct as election results roll in Tuesday evening.

While investors are signaling "begrudging acceptance" to a base-case scenario that there will indeed be a Democratic sweep, that's not how they were positioned, said Tobin Marcus, head of U.S. policy and politics at Wolfe Research. The firm's recent survey data among more than 100 institutional investors suggests they are not strongly positioned for any particular result.

"Investors seem to have a significant dose of skepticism about whether the final outcome is going to be quite as favorable to Democrats as the polling suggests," Marcus told CNBC, noting that the firm shares the view that the "polling is likely overstating Democratic strength."

JPMorgan sees a blue wave knocking the S&P 500 by about a half percent on Wednesday. Overnight futures were pointing to bigger losses than that on Wednesday.

On the other hand, if Republicans end up retaining both chambers of Congress, that could spur a rally, said Sameer Samana, head of global equities and real assets at the Wells Fargo Investment Institute.

Citigroup strategists said Monday that Republicans losing the House but managing to keep the Senate could be "a tailwind for equities into year-end." JPMorgan traders predict up to a 1% pop on Wednesday if Republicans hold on to that chamber. That is looking less likely.

Looking past any short-term move, stocks tend to rally after the midterms have concluded. In fact, the S&P 500 was up one year out after all midterm elections going back to the early 1980s.

But the S&P 500 has already recorded double-digit gains so far in 2026, so some on Wall Street worry that investors are being overly complacent heading into this year's elections.

During midterm election years, the S&P 500 has typically averaged a 14% drawdown at some point in the second half of the year found Jonathan Krinsky, chief market technician at BTIG.

"The reason stocks often perform well in November during midterms is because [the S&P 500] typically is weak INTO the elections. We have the opposite setup this year," Krinsky wrote last week. "Therefore, while consensus is that we see a post midterm rally, we think the opposite is actually more likely."

It's not helping that the broader market has recently come under significant pressure due to turbulence in the artificial intelligence trade, which led the Nasdaq Composite to post its worst day since June on Friday. AI shares led the market lower again on Monday.

A strong showing from Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections could pressure the trade even more.

At the state level, increased Democratic control is probably a headwind for stocks associated with data centers and technological capital equipment, analysts said.

"It [would solidify] the early kind of cautionary signs in different states around the advancement of AI infrastructure," Rob Rains at Washington Analysis told CNBC.

Goldman Sachs strategists said this week that a Democratic Congress "might limit data center tax incentives, but a national moratorium looks unlikely in any election outcome."

One example would be Texas — a key hub of the data center buildout. A surprise loss there by Republican Gov. Greg Abbott has the "potential to spook" the market in the short term, Wolfe's Marcus said.

A blue wave might pressure defense shares, while boosting healthcare stocks, analysts and investors said.

"A substantial Democratic sweep could limit real spending growth unless a broader agreement increases both defense and health-care funding," said Alex Sagal, global equity analyst at Wells Fargo Investment Institute. However, he noted that the earnings outlook for the defense industry looks "stable to modestly positive."

Wolfe Research's Marcus said that healthcare is going to be "the biggest focus," as Democrats will push for restoring expired Affordable Care Act enhanced premium subsidies or unwinding some of the Medicaid reforms in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act.

"Healthcare stocks have been doing really well lately," Jed Ellerbroek, portfolio manager at Argent Capital Management, said to CNBC. "I think part of that is the anti-AI trade, but I also think part of it is just optimism that Democrats are taking over."

Ellerbroek is keeping his eye on hospital-related stocks such as HCA Healthcare and Tenet Healthcare as well as health insurers like UnitedHealth and Centene.

Financials was the worst S&P 500 sector in early 2026 on the heels of President Donald Trump calling on Congress to pass a law that would cap credit card interest rates at 10%. While the sector has been able to notch gains since, with its year-to-date rise now at around 3%, things could get more unstable for the group under Democratic control.

"Democrats will come in wanting to show tangible progress on the affordability issue, and that means targeting sectors that are front and center in consumer budgets," said Evercore ISI's Matthew Aks. "We know Trump has populist instincts, populist leanings, and could he pick a couple of issues and decide to team up with the Democrats on those issues and target those industries in a populist way? Absolutely."

Given that Democratic control is often associated with increased regulation, while Republican control is thought to promote deregulation, Democrats could be interested in passing credit card rate caps if they come out victorious.

"I think if Democrats have control of one or both chambers, they would try to pass it and see if they could get Trump to either sign it or force him to veto it, and that would let Democrats kind of score a political point," Aks said.

Trump could restart the war against Iran after holding off on any major strikes leading up to the midterms, a move that could upend the overall market and cause energy stocks to outperform. Crude oil prices jumped more than 10% in October, though they gave a lot of that gain back on Monday.

Gasoline prices stood at $4.17 a gallon on average nationwide Sunday, a nearly 40% increase compared with the same period last year, according to AAA.

"If there are escalations, that could obviously put upward pressure on prices just ahead of Thanksgiving," said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at Gas Buddy, "which is also when Americans have really staunch traditions in terms of driving to friends and family."

If Trump decides to take forceful action, it could snuff out hope for a typical post-midterm year-end rally.

Evercore ISI said the S&P 500 could retreat by double digits to end the year at 6,800 as oil spikes if the president "moves to kinetic escalation in Iran ahead of seating the new Congress in January" and Democrats take Congress.

#2875. Eintracht claim second straight win over HSV

Eintracht Frankfurt saw off Hamburger SV in the final game of Bundesliga Matchday 8 on Sunday evening, securing a 3-1 win at Deutsche Bank Park.

It was the second consecutive victory over Die Rothosen for Eintracht, having beaten them 3-1 at the Volksparkstadion in the DFB-Pokal in midweek.

Die Adler took the lead just 10 minutes into the game when Warmed Omari inadvertently turned the ball into his own net while attempting to prevent it from reaching Younes Ebnoutalib at the back post.

However, HSV quickly hit back to equalise through Terem Moffi, who was picked out by Albert Grønbæk inside the box after Lillian Brassier failed to clear the ball properly.

Eintracht regained the lead early in the second half, with Raphael Onyedika finding the back of the net after Daniel Heuer Fernandes parried Brassier's header poorly.

After Fares Chaibi had a goal ruled out for offside, Eintracht Frankfurt eventually added a third, with Younes Ebnoutalib rounding off the scoring in the 69th minute to make it 3-1.

The result lifts Eintracht to eighth in the Bundesliga table, while HSV sit 15th.

#2870. Giannis Antetokounmpo believes Luka Doncic is one of the NBA's greatest-ever scorers

Giannis Antetokounmpo believes Luka Doncic is one of the NBA's greatest-ever scorers originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Luka Doncic has played seven games so far in the 2026-27 NBA season, and he has looked like the league's best player during this early-season stretch. He is averaging a whopping 38.3 points per game on an efficient 49.7% from the field and 42.7% from beyond the arc.

Despite his scoring, that does not mean that he is struggling with the rest of his game because Doncic is still chipping in with 8.0 rebounds and 8.1 assists per game. He has truly cemented himself as a complete player, but his scoring has definitely taken the cake for his biggest strength.

Doncic has been one of the league's best players for almost his entire career. He has cemented himself as one of the most talented players in the league, and thankfully, that is also seen in the raw numbers he is putting up.

In the Los Angeles Lakers' latest game, Doncic scored 42 points against the Miami Heat. Considering the Heat have a lot of solid defenders, performing like that just shows how good Doncic is as an offensive player.

MORE: Luka Doncic has revealed how he has improved his game ever since becoming the main man in Los Angeles

Former two-time MVP Giannis Antetokounmpo had a superb performance against the Lakers as well with 30 points, 12 rebounds, and seven assists. However, that was not enough to contain Doncic. In fact, after the game, Giannis was full of praise for his fellow superstar.

"Luka is one of the best scorers to ever play this game, if not the best. Not right now, not right now. Like, of all time. I feel like some people are scared to say that, but it's gotta be up there," Antetokounmpo asserted about Doncic's status as an all-time great scorer.

Giannis is making great points here because Luka has all of the makings of an all-timer. He has the arsenal of moves, the aura of an unstoppable scorer, and the numbers to boot. At the moment, Doncic is still in his prime, but his legacy might get even better when he retires.

#2869. Saints release former All-Pro kicker Daniel Carlson after another missed FG in loss to Steelers

The New Orleans Saints are in the market for a new kicker.

The Saints announced on Tuesday that they have released veteran kicker Daniel Carlson after Carlson missed another field goal in a 16-10 Week 7 loss to the Pittsburgh Steelers.

Carlson missed a 52-yard attempt with 9:42 remaining that would have cut Pittsburgh's lead to 16-13. He finished the game hitting 1-of-2 field goal attempts, dropping his season success rate on field goals to a career-low 66.7% (8 of 12).

Carlson hit all of his field goals up to 49 yards with the Saints, but was 3 of 7 on attempts from 50-plus yards. His release ends his tenure with the Saints after seven games.

Head coach Kellen Moore addressed the team's kicking issues during his Monday news conference, foreshadowing Carlson's release.

"Obviously we just got to continue to evaluate the field goal opportunities," Moore said, per ESPN. "And so that'd be something that we'll just have to work through and discuss and find the best solution for Sunday."

Carlson, 31, is a nine-year NFL veteran who was named All-Pro twice with the Raiders in 2021 and 2022. He spent seven-plus seasons with the Raiders before joining the Saints in the offseason.

The Saints are coming off a Week 8 bye and will need to replace Carlson before facing the Cleveland Browns on Sunday. New Orleans comes out of its bye at 2-5, in last place in the NFC South.

The Saints have Charlie Smyth on their practice squad and could return to him if they don't sign another kicker. Smyth hit 12-of-16 field goal attempts in six games with the Saints in 2025 in his only NFL experience. The Saints signed Carlson after Smyth failed to win the job in training camp.

#2867. Yahoo Fantasy Football Expert League Recap: JSN's quiet second half sinks Winks while Harmon gets right in Week 8

If you're unfamiliar with the Yahoo Fantasy Football Expert League, let's fill you in a bit before we go over Week 8. Yahoo Fantasy houses some of the leading analysts in the industry, and we were fortunate enough to get all of them in a league for the 2026 NFL season. To keep you up to date on things, we'll be providing weekly recaps on all the action. Let's get into it.

Here's a quick look at what the standings are as we approach the midway point of the fantasy season:

Here's a look at the Yahoo expert league standings after Week 8.

It was a week that featured plenty of very tight matchups, including three that were decided by five or fewer fantasy points. Let's look at those before we talk about how Matt Harmon's team went atomic.

We'll start up top with Scott Pianowski. Say what you want about his team (he's already disparaged it himself), but it just keeps winning. Pianowski improved to 7-1 to move firmly into first place in the Expert League. He sent Chris Allen to a whopping seventh straight loss with a 104.92-100.5 victory. It came down to MNF with Pianowski having Luther Burden III and Colston Loveland, and Allen rolling out D'Andre Swift.

What's so fascinating about this matchup is all the busts. For Allen, he unfortunately had Lamar Jackson. But the injury to Lamar also hurt Pianowski, who played Zay Flowers. Chris Godwin Jr. gave Allen nothing as the Bucs didn't complete a pass, while Scott got 0.9 points from Keon Coleman.

Swift's three-rushing-TD explosion wasn't enough to propel Allen into the win column. Pianowski survives and gets some help elsewhere to claim first place.

That elsewhere was in the Hayden Winks-Eric Edholm matchup, which turned into another exciting finish. Going into MNF, Edholm held a 131.38-108.58 lead over Winks. Normally, that would be comfortable enough to feel good if you're Edholm. But on the other side, he was staring down Jaxon Smith-Njigba.

If you asked Edholm at halftime of Bears-Seahawks how he felt about his chances of winning, he'd likely tell you #notgood. JSN posted four catches for 92 yards and a score in the first half against Chicago. But this is fantasy football, and nothing is ever as it seems.

Smith-Njigba would catch two passes for 26 yards in the second half, leaving Winks exactly two fantasy points short of tying. We already had one tie earlier in the season in this league; two would have been wild. Instead, Edholm improved to 5-3 on the season with his third straight victory.

The other close matchup was less impactful overall for the standings as Dan Titus was able to take down Eric Froton, 104-101.78.

Now, we return to Harmon getting back in the groove. After scoring just 66 points last week in a key matchup with Winks, Harmon was the high scorer for Week 8 with 150.10 to easily dispatch Josh Norris. Tyler Warren was the only player who didn't reach double-digit fantasy points for Harmon. Norris did have Sam Darnold and the Seahawks D/ST going into MNF but that didn't play a factor at all.

Looking ahead to Week 9, we have some pretty big matchups, including Harmon-Edholm, Pianowski-Max Loeb and Justin Boone-Winks.

#2866. Stephen Curry Sends Out 5-Word Post Amid Warriors’ Winning Streak

SAN FRANCISCO, CALIFORNIA - NOVEMBER 02: Stephen Curry #30 of the Golden State Warriors reacts after making a three-point shot against the Phoenix Suns in the second half of their game at Chase Center on November 02, 2026 in San Francisco, California. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Thearon W. Henderson/Getty Images) Getty

CHARLOTTE, NORTH CAROLINA - MARCH 03: Stephen Curry #30 of the Golden State Warriors looks on during the second half of a basketball game against the Charlotte Hornets at Spectrum Center on March 03, 2025 in Charlotte, North Carolina. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by David Jensen/Getty Images) Getty

The Golden State Warriors are one of the hottest teams in the NBA right now.

The Warriors started the season slowly at 0-3, but they have now won five in a row after completing a 17-point comeback against the Phoenix Suns on Monday.

Stephen Curry is putting up MVP numbers at the age of 38. He is averaging 30.0 points, 4.2 rebounds, 4.5 assists and 1.3 steals in under 30 minutes per game.

GettyStephen Curry #30 of the Golden State Warriors reacts after making a three-point shot against the Phoenix Suns in the second half of their game at Chase Center on November 02, 2026 in San Francisco, California.

In an Instagram post on Tuesday, Stephen Curry shared images and reels from their last two wins against the LA Clippers and Phoenix Suns.

Curry highlighted himself, as well as his teammates like Yaxel Lendeborg, Brandon Williams, Brandin Podziemski, De'Anthony Melton, Charles Bassey, Georges Niang and L.J. Cryer, among others.

The two-time MVP also send out a five-word message about their early season success.

"Starting to find that rhythm," Curry wrote.

There were plenty of doubts surrounding the Warriors entering the season. They weren't the most active team in free agency, and trade rumors were creeping up about Curry's future with the franchise.

However, Curry remained committed to the Warriors and signed a two-year, $116 million extension that will keep him in the Bay Area until the 2028-29 season.

Lendeborg's play as a rookie has been one of the main positives for the Warriors. He has proven that there might be a future for the franchise after the Curry era.

GettyStephen Curry #30 of the Golden State Warriors looks on during the second half of a basketball game against the Charlotte Hornets at Spectrum Center on March 03, 2025 in Charlotte, North Carolina.

Despite his age, Stephen Curry is putting up numbers never seen before.

According to Carson Breber of Nerd Sesh, Curry is having the best scoring season for a 38-year-old point guard or older. He is averaging 30.0 points per game, which would be the highest mark for a 38-year-old PG in NBA history.

"No 38-year-old point guard has ever even averaged 12 PPG in a season. 38-year-old Steph Curry is currently averaging 30.0 PPG (in 28.8 MPG) on 68.8% TS. He's been a Top 3 player so far this season. Do not take for granted the absurdity of what he's doing," Breber tweeted.

Of course, the Warriors are doing their part in managing Curry's minutes. His age isn't the only factor since he missed nearly half of last season due to runner's knee.

If the Warriors want to continue their success, Curry's health is going to be their top priority.

And if they can replicate their success when Curry sits out or rests and acquire another center, the Warriors might have a chance at making the postseason.

#2865. Why ESPN will have trouble landing Peyton Manning to replace Troy Aikman on ‘MNF’

ESPN's preferred replacement for Troy Aikman is already connected to the network — but is an unlikely answer to their vacancy.

During a Q&A Tuesday, The Athletic's Andrew Marchand said he believes Peyton Manning is "ESPN's No. 1 choice" to team with NFL play-by-play voice Joe Buck when Aikman's contract is up after the Super Bowl in February.

Peyton Manning arrives for the premiere of Hulu's "Chad Powers" at Rose Bowl Stadium in Pasadena, California, Thursday, Sept. 25, 2025. Getty Images

Manning has previously turned down offers to work in a broadcast booth. Still, it is likely ESPN president Jimmy Pitaro will reach out to Manning to gauge his interest on the idea, according to Marchand.

He and his Omaha production company are under a longterm contract with ESPN for his and brother Eli Manning's "Manning-Cast" that airs during most "Monday Night Football" broadcasts.

The sibling quarterback greats have an ideal situation with the Worldwide Leader, where they can work from home and cover games without the grueling travel schedule analysts endure.

The Mannings are scheduled to analyze 12 games this season, as well as Super Bowl 2027, The Athletic previously reported.

Joe Buck, left, and Troy Aikman look on before an NFC Wild Card playoff game between the Dallas Cowboys and Tampa Bay Buccaneers in Tampa, Fla., Monday, Jan. 16, 2023. Getty Images

Buck recently moved to Denver and lives five minutes from Peyton, according to The Athletic.

Greg Olsen, who is a color commentator in Fox's No. 2 booth, is considered the favorite for the job, per Marchand.

Aikman and Buck have been at odds in the booth after Buck signed a six-year contract extension with ESPN in September, worth more than $100 million, to continue "Monday Night Football."

Troy Aikman looks on from the sideline during warmups prior to an NFL football game between the Dallas Cowboys and the Philadelphia Eagles at Lincoln Financial Field on October 26, 2026 in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. Getty Images

During a Sept. 20 interview on the "Sports Media with Richard Deitsch" podcast, Aikman explained that he thought he would've had a new contract done — and he hadn't heard from ESPN at that point.

Aikman and Buck have been broadcast partners for 25 years between their time at Fox and ESPN.

Both Buck and Aikman — who called more than 300 games together for Fox — left the network for ESPN to step into the "Monday Night Football" booth in March 2022.

#2864. Ian Rapoport Gives Update on WR Who Rejected Trade to San Francisco 49ers

The San Francisco 49ers have already made one blockbuster trade ahead of the Nov. 10 deadline, acquiring DeForest Buckner from the Indianapolis Colts.

It also appears they nearly completed another major move, which came to light over the weekend.

Fox Sports' Jay Glazer reported that the 49ers were working on a deal to bring back former wide receiver Jauan Jennings, who's currently with the Minnesota Vikings.

The two sides seemingly had a deal in place, but it hinged on whether Jennings wanted to return to San Francisco. He ultimately rejected the idea, and the trade fell through.

During an appearance on the "Pat McAfee Show" on Tuesday, ESPN's Ian Rapoport offered his perspective on the situation and addressed whether he believed Glazer's report was accurate. He seemed hesitant to confirm that Jennings rejected the trade.

"I know there was definitely talk between the two sides," Rapoport said. "I know that there was a time that I thought a deal was gonna get done. Thought it was gonna happen. I had a nice tweet ready and then it fell apart. I haven't definitively heard that it was Jennings scuttling it. That would seem a little odd to me, but I guess it's possible."

Now in his first season with the Vikings, Jennings has been a nonfactor in their offense despite arriving as the team's No. 3 wide receiver behind Justin Jefferson and Jordan Addison.

That makes it surprising that he would reportedly turn down a return to San Francisco, where he spent the first five years of his NFL career and found considerable success in head coach Kyle Shanahan's offense.

During his time with the 49ers, Jennings played in 75 games and recorded 210 receptions for 2,581 yards and 22 touchdowns.

#2863. NFL news, live updates: Ravens name undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano starting QB; Cardinals RB Jeremiyah Love headed to IR with ankle injury; Lions LB Jack Campbell remains hospitalized with neck injury

Week 8 of the 2026 NFL season is nearly a wrap, with just the Chicago Bears and Seattle Seahawks left to play on "Monday Night Football."

There was plenty of excitement on Sunday, including two games that went into overtime, but they unfortunately weren't without injuries. Baltimore Ravens quarterback Lamar Jackson made his return to the lineup after rehabbing an ankle injury, but that excitement was short-lived, as he suffered a thumb injury on his first pass attempt of the game. Per head coach Jesse Minter, he'll require surgery and a stint on the injured reserve list — a brutal blow.

Backup Tyler Huntley is also dealing with a turf toe injury, which forced Baltimore to resort to their emergency third quarterback, Joe Fagnano. He completed 10-of-21 passes for 91 yards and an interception in relief of Jackson and Huntley.

The Ravens are playing on a short week, as they're set to host the Jacksonville Jaguars on "Thursday Night Football" in Week 9, and Fagnano will officially be QB1 for that matchup. The 25-year-old, who played his college ball at Maine and then UConn, will be the second undrafted rookie to start under center for Baltimore in the Ravens' 32-year history, per ESPN's Jamison Hensley.

The Detroit Lions handed the Minnesota Vikings their first loss of the season, but lost a key playmaker on defense as All-Pro linebacker Jack Campbell suffered a scary-looking neck injury. He was immobilized on a backboard before being transported to the hospital as his teammates gathered around him in an emotional scene.

Follow along with Yahoo Sports for the latest NFL news, injury updates and more coming out of Week 8.

The Colts aren't purging their entire roster.

Per NFL Network, the Colts and defensive lineman Adetomiwa Adebawore have agreed to a $35 million contract extension with a $13 million roster bonus.

The deal adds $22.5 million in new money to his contract through the 2027 season, per the report. Adebawore was previously on track to became a free agent in the offseason at the conclusion of his four-year, $4.6 million rookie contract.

Adebawore, 25, has tied his career high with four sacks through eight games in addition to 19 tackles and eight quarterback hits.

The New Orleans Saints are on the kicker market.

The Saints announced on Tuesday that they have released veteran kicker Daniel Carlson after Carlson missed another field goal in a 16-10 Week 7 loss to the Pittsburgh Steelers.

Carlson missed a 52-yard attempt with 9:42 remaining that would have cut Pittsburgh's lead to 16-13. He finished the game hitting 1 of 2 field-goal attempts, dropping his season success rate on field goals to a career-low 66.7% (8 of 12).

For more on the Saints' kicker search, read here.

The Ravens injury woes continue, as the team placed kicker Tyler Loop on the injured reserve list with a groin injury on Tuesday.

Loop has made 13 of 17 field goals attempted this season, having gone 5 for 5 on kicks from 50-plus yards. He missed one field goal in Week 8's loss to the Buffalo Bills from 34 yards.

The team signed kicker Jake Moody to the practice squad on Oct. 20, and he'll presumably step up in Loop's absence.

If officially elevated to the active roster, the Ravens will be Moody's fourth team over the past two seasons. He hopped between the San Francisco 49ers (who selected him in the third round of the 2023 NFL Draft), Chicago Bears and Washington Commanders last season, good on 19 of 23 attempts in 2025.

The Buffalo Bills have released safety C.J. Gardner-Johnson, NFL insider Jordan Schultz reported on Tuesday. The team then confirmed the move on social media.

Gardner-Johnson served a one-game suspension this past week after spitting on Las Vegas Raiders tight end in Week 6, an action that led to his ejection. The Bills then had a bye in Week 7, which led to him serving that suspension in Week 8.

Gardner-Johnson has been a bit of a journeyman in recent seasons, with the Bills being his sixth team in eight seasons. He started his career with the New Orleans Saints, who drafted him in the fourth round out of Florida. He's also had stints with the Philadelphia Eagles, Chicago Bears, Detroit Lions and Houston Texans.

He started in all six games of his games played for the Bills this past season. Damar Halmin got the start in Gardner Johnson's place while he served his suspension.

Baltimore Ravens head coach Jesse Minter told the media on Tuesday that the team would start undrafted rookie quarterback Joe Fagnano on Thursday against the Jacksonville Jaguars.

The announcement comes as starting quarterback Lamar Jackson is headed to injured reserve with a thumb injury and backup quarterback Tyler Huntley was diagnosed with turf toe.

Fagnano will be the second undrafted rookie to start under center for Baltimore in the Ravens' 32-year history, per ESPN's Jamison Hensley.

The rookie finished his collegiate career at UConn. He played in 24 games with the Huskies and ended his time with them having accumulated 5,252 passing yards and 48 touchdowns. Fagnano is less mobile than Jackson or Huntley and rushed for just 223 yards and 4 touchdowns on 93 carries at UConn.

Although Baltimore Ravens quarterback Joe Fagnano was spotted wearing a guard/brace on his right ankle Tuesday, as captured by The Athletic's Jeff Zrebiec, he was practicing, two days out from a "Thursday Night Football" game against the Jacksonville Jaguars.

Lamar Jackson (thumb) and Tyler Huntley (toe) have already been ruled out for the matchup. Jackson, of course, could be done for the season.

Huntley is navigating what's reportedly thought to be turf toe.

So Fagnano, a 25-year-old undrafted rookie who played his college ball at UConn and Maine, could make his first career NFL start this week, in primetime no less.

Fagnano was listed as a limited participant in the Ravens' walkthrough on Monday. Baltimore signed fellow QB Austin Reed to the practice squad that day.

Arizona Cardinals rookie running back Jeremiyah Love is headed to injured reserve with a right high-ankle sprain, NFL Network's Ian Rapoport reports.

The injury and IR designation will sideline Love for a minimum of four games. The Cardinals have upcoming games against the Seahawks, Rams, Chiefs and Commanders.

Love injured his ankle in Sunday's win over the Dallas Cowboys when his ankle got rolled underneath his body on a tackle. He was able to get up to walk to the sideline, but he did not return to the game as the Cardinals rallied from a 14-3 halftime deficit for a 33-30 overtime win.

Trade rumors have surrounded Arizona Cardinals defensive end Josh Sweat for a while now. NFL insider Jordan Schultz reported Tuesday that the belief around the league is that the 29-year-old Sweat would be open to a trade.

That said, per Schultz, Arizona's asking price is believed to be high, and the team has given the impression that it'd be OK holding onto the pass rusher.

Sweat is in his second season with the Cardinals, who signed him to a four-year contract in free agency last year, a deal reportedly worth $76.4 million, including $41 million guaranteed.

When Sweat arrived in Arizona, he was reuniting with then-Cardinals head coach Jonathan Gannon, who was previously Sweat's defensive coordinator with the Philadelphia Eagles. But the Cardinals moved on from Gannon after last season. That coaching change raised questions about Sweat's future in the desert.

During the 2025 season, Sweat piled up 12 sacks. He got off to a slow start this time around but has logged 19 total tackles and three sacks in his past five games.

If the Broncos decide they are on a heater in which the coin is repeatedly flipping on their side for a statistically improbable reason, they have some work to do before the trade deadline on Nov. 10. The offense, which has been inconsistent all season and was catching boos from the home crowd Sunday after three straight three-and-outs to start the second half, should be the focus.

Denver is just 30th in yards gained this season. A play-calling change from offensive coordinator Davis Webb to Payton hasn't helped much. The Broncos aren't getting a quarterback to replace Bo Nix but practically every other position group could be a candidate for an upgrade. The defense is good enough for a long playoff run, as it was last season. The variable is the offense.

Or maybe it just doesn't matter. The Broncos have beaten the odds for two seasons. They're 20-5 since the start of the 2025 season and 16 of those wins have been by eight points or less. They continue to laugh at the regression proclamations.

QB3 Joe Fagnano, who finished Sunday's loss to the Bills, was limited during Monday's walkthrough with an ankle injury.

Baltimore has a short week as they take on the Jaguars Thursday night.

The Los Angeles Rams pushed a lot of chips to the middle of the table over the offseason. Then, when they came out of the gates slow, other teams saw an opportunity.

Last week, the Seattle Seahawks made a big move for guard Quenton Nelson and the San Francisco 49ers got a big acquisition in defensive lineman DeForest Buckner. The NFC West got a lot harder.

The Chicago Bears, clearly believing the NFC is up for grabs, traded a couple first-round picks to the Tennessee Titans for defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons. His stellar play on Monday night in his Bears debut has lifted Chicago's ceiling for this season. Other top NFC teams will inevitably add to their rosters.

The Rams need to look to see if they have any more chips remaining to up their bet.

A pair of Cowboys defensive backs are now in concussion protocol, and linebacker Malachi Lawrence is dealing with an ankle injury.

It's unlcear how long any of them will be sidelined just yet.

Cardinals wide receiver Kendrick Bourne sustained a fractured tibia during Sunday's win over the Cowboys, ESPN's Adam Schefter reports.

The injury will end Bourne's season. It won't require surgery, per the report.

In eight games this season, Bourne has 26 catches for 263 yards with no touchdowns.

Cardinals rookie Jeremiyah love seemingly can't catch a break on the injury front, with head coach Mike LaFleur telling reporters on Monday that the rookie has suffered another ankle injury.

Love missed multiple preseason games with a high right ankle sprain, and missed two games last month with a left ankle injury. However, LaFleur said that the injury Love sustained on Sunday was a new injury, not a re-aggravation of one of his previous knocks.

"This is new, so this is not the left ankle or the right ankle, it's a new portion of the ankle," LaFleur said.

Love exited Sunday's win over the Cowboys after his ankle got rolled under his body on a tackle in the first half. The rookie running back was able to walk to the sideline but slammed his helmet down, clearly frustrated, before heading into the locker room; he did not return to the game.

LaFleur said that he'll give reporters more information on Wednesday, adding that he wanted to get a chance to talk to Love first.

Myles Garrett will return to practice for the Los Angeles Rams this week. Head coach Sean McVay announced the news to reporters Monday.

Garrett's played just one game for the Rams since the blockbuster offseason trade that sent him to Los Angeles from the Cleveland Browns. He underwent knee surgery after struggling to make an impact in the Rams' season-opening loss to the 49ers in Australia and has been sidelined since.

Garrett's planned return to practice doesn't mean he'll be ready to play Sunday against the Washington Commanders. But it's a positive sign for his return to the playing field.

His return to practice from injured reserve opens a 21-day window for Garrett to be elevated to the active roster or placed on season-ending injured reserve.

Eagles head coach Nick Sirianni spoke to the media on Monday. He said he isn't making a change at offensive coordinator, and Sean Mannion will remain in the role.

Philly has lost six straight games. The Eagles took a 10-0 lead over the Commanders on Monday night, but Washington scored 27 consecutive points to win.

Injuries are piling up for Philadelphia. The Eagles are expected to be without starting quarterback Jalen Hurts for the remainder of the season, leaving Tanner McKee currently under center. Saquon Barkley has been nursing a hamstring injury, and Philly was without DeVonta Smith on Monday night.

Arizona Cardinals head coach Mike LaFleur wasn't ready to commit to a starting quarterback on Sunday afternoon, even if his backup just led them to a wild overtime win against the Dallas Cowboys.

But just 24 hours after that victory, he made the move. Gardner Minshew is their starter moving forward.

LaFleur opted to bench Jacoby Brissett for Minshew on Sunday afternoon at AT&T Stadium, a decision that eventually led to their 33-30 overtime win against the Cowboys. LaFleur declined to commit to either quarterback when asked directly after the game, even though the expectation was that Minshew would remain in the role.

"I'm just excited about this win right now," he said, via ESPN.

Now, Minshew will get the start against the Seattle Seahawks on Sunday. Carson Beck will serve as his backup, and Brissett will be the team's third-string quarterback.

The Baltimore Ravens are in need of at least one more quarterback and now have one on their practice squad. They signed Austin Reed to their practice squad on Monday.

Backup Tyler Huntley suffered a toe injury in the same game that sidelined him before halftime. His prognosis was not yet clear as of Monday afternoon. Third-string emergency quarterback Joe Fagnano finished the 36-10 loss for the Ravens. He completed 10 of 21 passes for 91 yards with an interception and no touchdowns.

Reed, 26, has never thrown an NFL pass. He played two seasons at Western Kentucky in 2022 and 2023 and spent part of training camp this summer with the Ravens.

The Ravens are facing a short with with a Thursday night game against the Jacksonville Jaguars.

Micah Parsons will be back at Packers practice this week in a significant step in his return from a torn ACL.

Head coach Matt LaFleur announced the news on Monday. It remains unclear when Parsons will make his 2026 season debut.

Parsons tore his left ACL and damaged his meniscus in Week 15 last season against the Denver Broncos on Dec. 14. His return to the field is taking longer than anticipated.

#2860. Penn State’s successful ground game against Purdue stems from bye week

Three weeks ago, Penn State football coach Matt Campbell and offensive coordinator Taylor Mouser sounded frustrated with the offense in general and the running game in particular.

The Nittany Lions' struggles continued the following week against Michigan before their bye week. The coaches spent the next two weeks pushing the offensive line to be more physical and searching to find the right combination at running back.

After averaging 75 rushing yards in its first four Big Ten games, Penn State ran for 269 in its 31-17 victory over Purdue last weekend.

"I would say I was desperate," Mouser said Tuesday. "When you're not winning here and you're not living up to the standard, you feel like you're letting a lot more people down than just the players and the coaches.

"For those guys to go out there and play with that level of physicality, violence and conviction, to be able to see real chemistry and real communication at the line, we looked like a real football team at times in the game."

It was the second-highest rushing total by Penn State this season, falling short of the 304 yards it had against Buffalo. It also was just the fourth time in the last 15 games against an opponent from a Power Four conference that a Campbell-coached team scored at least 30 points.

Cam Wallace, who was in effect the fourth-team running back, impressed Campbell and Mouser in practice the last two weeks to earn his first career start. He rushed 22 times for 135 yards, both career highs, after getting just 22 carries in the first seven games.

"What you guys saw Saturday was what we saw on the practice field the last two weeks," Campbell said about Wallace. "It was cut and dried. It was easy. When it's not cut and dried, it's really hard to make tough decisions when everybody's competing.

"When you leave it gray, we can make mistakes. When you don't leave it gray, you leave yourself in a great situation and then you capitalize on it."

Besides Wallace, Carson Hansen ran 17 times for a season-high 96 yards and a touchdown.

"I'm really proud of Cam and Carson and the way those guys ran," Mouser said. "We really challenged the whole running back room. To be able to have somebody draw a line in the sand and say, 'I'm going to be the guy,' those guys did a great job during the bye week and left no doubt.

"When it wasn't perfect, Cam was running at the level that our running backs have to play at from a physicality, attitude and effort standpoint."

Penn State's offensive line played its best game of the season, perhaps not coincidentally against a defense that ranked 97th or worse nationally in every major category. Its performance came after the coaches concentrated on improving the linemen's physicality.

The last two weeks, they worked on blocking double teams in the running game for 20-minute clips during the period usually set aside for individual drills.

"I wanted them to buy more into the mentality of what it takes to run the ball," Mouser said. "It doesn't necessarily matter what the calls are. Those guys have to be excited and sprint to the line of scrimmage. It's going to be physical. It's going to be violent. It's going to be downhill.

"We had really, really physical bye week practices. It wasn't an off week for our guys. I was probably more proud that we had the ball for 43 minutes than anything else."

Penn State (1-4 Big Ten, 4-4) will face a stronger defense when it meets Washington (2-3, 5-3) Saturday at 3:30 (TV-FS1) in Seattle. The Huskies rank 42nd in scoring defense and 54th in total defense and rushing defense.

Campbell hopes that simplifying the offense during the bye week will help the Lions have success again this week.

"Sometimes as coaches – and I'm at fault for this as much as anybody – you're trying to over-scheme to put yourself in the best position at the moment," he said. "When you can sit down and digest it all, you say, 'OK, this is what we're best at. Let's make sure we're teaching what we can do best.'

"That really has been the emphasis since the USC game. We had a little more time to attack it during the bye week. We were able to find a little bit better rhythm in terms of how we want to run the football."

#2856. Deuce's Deep Dives: Kings' Darius Acuff Jr., rookies making early NBA impacts

Deuce's Deep Dives: Kings' Darius Acuff Jr., rookies making early NBA impacts originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

One of the biggest storylines heading into the 2026-27 NBA season was the talented rookie draft class, and they have not disappointed through the first few weeks.

Five rookies currently are averaging over 17 points per game, and a number of top draft picks are having immediate impacts on their teams.

AJ Dybantsa, the No. 1 overall pick, is averaging 19 points per game for an improved Washington Wizards team, while No. 3 overall pick Cameron Boozer has been an all-around force for the 6-1 Memphis Grizzlies.

Versatile forwards Caleb Wilson and Yaxel Lendeborg, both lottery picks, are heavily featured in their teams' offenses, but it is Sacramento Kings guard Darius Acuff Jr. that leads this draft class, scoring 26.1 points per game.

On "Deuce's Deep Dives," NBC Sports California's Deuce Mason illustrated how Acuff's play is electrifying the Kings' fanbase.

"You're seeing the impacts from Yaxel to Caleb to Cam, and then we bring you to Sacramento with Darius Acuff Jr. giving Kings fans something to be excited about," Mason said. "Acuff is averaging 26 points per game. That is top 10 in the NBA in scoring, and it's early, but it's also ahead of some absolute titans of the game. We're talking about more points a game than Victor Wembanyama, than [Nikola Jokic], Cade Cunningham and Jalen Brunson, and he's only 19 years old."

Acuff has scored 20 or more points in five games this season, and 30 or more in three, ranking second in the NBA with 23.6 field goal attempts a night.

The rookie's efficiency leaves more to be desired, shooting 40.6 percent from the field and 31.3 percent on 9.6 3-pointers attempted per game, and his defense clearly needs improvement.

However, it's not Acuff's raw statistics that excite Mason, but the skills he has shown when leading Sacramento's offense.

"The numbers are only part of what makes Acuff so impressive," Mason added. "It's his handle. It's his change of pace. His ability to get to his spots. And even when that shot doesn't drop, it doesn't faze him. He keeps going. Defenses have thrown everything at him at this point: full court pressure, physicality, length, size, doubles. It doesn't seem to rattle him. He has a patience to his game that you do not expect from someone his age. Darius Acuff Jr. is becoming must-watch, and this rookie class is special."

The Kings won their first game of the season 115-100 over the Dallas Mavericks on Monday, with Acuff dropping 25 points and six assists alongside Zach LaVine's 23 points and Malik Monk's 24 points.

Sacramento appears unlikely to make the NBA playoffs after its 1-6 start, but with Acuff leading the charge, fans are getting a preview of the team's future.

#2849. Thunder sign Dalen Terry out of G League to provide depth at forward

Terry was in training camp with the Golden State Warriors and was set to report to their G League affiliate when the Thunder called.

Bringing in Terry helps the Thunder with depth in two ways. First, while he is not going to play a significant role, Terry can give them some wing minutes. Plus, by filling the 15th spot on the roster, the Thunder can use their two-way players without the restrictions of the "under-15" rule.

The Thunder are without three starters due to significant injuries. All-NBA wing Jalen Williams is out with a fractured fibula and dislocated ankle, while All-Defense guard Cason Wallace is dealing with a torn labrum. The Thunder also are without All-NBA big man Chet Holmgren due to a right knee patellar tear, but he is expected to return sometime after Christmas (likely in early 2027).

#2846. Bears vs. Seahawks Game Balls: The Nation Takes Notice

The Chicago Bears can win a Super Bowl. This year. The team left no doubt about that fact on Monday night at Seattle's Lumen Field.

The Bears thoroughly dominated the defending Super Bowl champions, in their own house, from bell to bell. This happening just one week after defeating last year's Super Bowl runner-up New Englad Patriots.

Also one week ago, the Seahawks defense kept Patrick Mahomes and the Kansas City Chiefs out of the end zone entirely. This week, the Bears went on a methodical, 17-play drive to score a touchdown on their first possession of the game against that same defense.

In defeating the Seahawks, as well as the Patriots last week, the Bears became the first team in NFL history to defeat both of the previous year's Super Bowl teams in back-to-back weeks. That's a heck of a stat.

Despite most of the NFL world now acknowledging the fact that Williams is a top tier quarterback, it feels like Monday night's game took that one step further. As I browse social media and the usual talking heads throughout the sports world, I'm starting to see a general consensus now that not only is #18 top-tier, he is elite. As in, a top five quarterback in the league.

A Chicago Bears quarterback. Elite. We've been waiting a long time for this, and we absolutely deserve it.

Williams did something to the Seahawks that no other QB has done this year – throw for 200 yards. And the Seahawks have played some pretty accomplished QBs and big names this year: Mahomes, Brock Purdy, Drake Maye, Justin Herbert, and Bo Nix to name a few. None of them accomplished this feat.

It took Williams barely half the game, surpassing 200 yards less than five minutes into the third quarter. And he did so on one of the most spectacular plays of the game.

The Bears' QB finished the night 24/34, 247 yards passing, one touchdown, zero interceptions, and a passer rating of 101.0.

You've got to think the Williams has firmly entered the MVP discussion and will remain there for the rest of the season.

Did the Bears give up too much for Simmons? If I'm the Titans, I'm thinking they didn't give up enough.

It can't be overstated how much of a difference Big Jeff made for the Bears defense from the very first play he was on the field. Those bright orange sleeves might as well have been a flashing warning sign to the rest of the league for the remainder of this season. Not only do the Bears have an elite offense; they now have a defense that opposing teams will (and should) fear.

That's a combination that wins championships.

In commanding so much attention from the offensive line, Simmons helped other Bears defenders in the front four look like All Pro candidates. Montez Sweat was a beast. Austin Booker continued to look the part of up and coming young star. Hell, James Lynch even had an interception, catching a ball that Booker tipped late in the game.

Simmons was on the field for 48 plays, and the Seahawks were held to just 3.0 yards per play (including three sacks and an interception) during those snaps. There were eight plays that Simmons was not on the field, and Seattle averaged 13.3 yards per play during those snaps. That kind of says it all.

Simmons finished the night with six tackles, one sack, two tackles for loss, one pass defended, and one QB hit.

And this is just the beginning. Simmons had only been with the team for a little bit over 72 hours on Monday night. Expect this train to keep rolling ahead at full speed in the week's ahead.

It hasn't been this exciting to be a Bears fan in a very long time. Relish it.

#2845. Steph Curry’s historic streak ends at worst possible time in Warriors thriller

SAN FRANCISCO — Stephen Curry had stepped to the foul line 50 times this season with the same result. Swish. Bottom of the net. Put a point on the board.

So, when he was fouled with the Warriors leading by two and less than six seconds left Monday night against the Suns, the game had to be as good as over … right?

Only after Curry clanked a free throw for the first time this season. Dillon Brooks grabbed the rebound and passed it ahead to Collin Gillespie, who got off a last-second heave from 36 feet.

It was a clean look, albeit an unlikely one, and could have sent the game to overtime. The shot fell short and Curry's ill-timed miss proved insignificant.

Stephen Curry of the Golden State Warriors looks on during a game against the Phoenix Suns on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Chase Center in San Francisco. NBAE via Getty Images

But in a game that he finished with 34 total — his third in a row with 30 or more — it was the one point Curry left on the board that drew the attention.

"I mean, he's just made so many big free throws in his career," coach Steve Kerr said. "Obviously he makes all of those. The law of averages catches up every once in a while."

Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry (30) shoots against the Phoenix Suns in an NBA basketball game in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jed Jacobsohn

In addition to being the greatest 3-point shooter in history, it would make sense then that Curry keeps the same standard in a controlled setting, 8 feet, 9 inches closer to the basket. His 91.3% career rate makes him the most efficient foul shooter of all time.

Curry is also responsible for two of the NBA's four longest stretches of success from the stripe to begin a season. With his streak, he was closing in on himself (52 in a row in 2017-18) for third place and Steve Nash (53 in 2007-08) for second for the most foul shots without a miss.

It would have taken some time to snatch the record away from Jose Calderon (84 in 2008-09).

But with a chance to put the game on ice and move into a tie for third place, for the first time in a competitive game in 204 days, Curry's shot clanked off the rim, into the hands of Brooks.

Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry shoots against Phoenix Suns guard Devin Booker and center Khaman Maluach in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jed Jacobsohn

Fortunately for the Warriors, the 11 free throws, five 3s and four shots inside the arc that did find the bottom of the net proved to be just enough in the 116-113 win, their fifth in a row.

While Curry's free throw streak came to an end at 51, he also extended another with his 34-point performance. At age 38 and 233 days, only two older players in NBA history have dropped 30 three games in a row: LeBron James and Michael Jordan.

One miss in a vast ocean of splashes, of course, did nothing to shake Curry's confidence.

"I'm shocked," Curry smirked, "when I miss anytime."
