#2844. The Cowboys and the cruel art of collapse

The Dallas Cowboys are bad at football. That's a tough reality to live in. For months we have been clinging to the hope that a stellar offense with a gradually improving defense would be the magic ticket to help this team pick up steam and start rolling down the stretch of the season. We have some bad news. Nothing's rolling.

The defense remains terrible, and the offense has started to join in by underperforming at unpopular moments in the game. The whole thing culminates in disappointing outings and losses to what we think are inferior teams. One after the other.

The latest letdown elevates the frustration to another notch. That's because it appeared like they were onto something by building a 14-point lead, but things inevitably fell apart. What happened this time? Taking a closer look paints an uncomfortable picture.

To start off, it's important to note that these two teams aren't too far off when it comes to ability. They both now have the same record, and this one went down to the wire, so it tracks. That's why it was misleading when the Cowboys got off to an early lead. It wasn't because of sheer dominance. It was just luck.

When you look at what went on early in the game, the Cowboys benefited greatly when a Jonathan Mingo muffed punt bounced right back into his hands. They were also fortunate that backup tight end James Mitchell was right there to fall on a Javonte Williams fumble in the end zone. Even Shemar James' interception that he returned deep into Arizona territory bounced right into his arms after it deflected off the Cardinals' receiver. You could make a strong case that this game would've remained a lot tighter had the ball bounced a little differently.

But hey, that's football, and Cowboys fans certainly aren't going to complain when some breaks go their way. The Cardinals kept dropping passes, committed a lot of false starts at terrible times, and even shanked the game-winning extra point at the end of regulation. Bad teams do bad things, and the Cardinals did their fair share.

Offensively, you might be inclined to think the Cowboys scored 30 points and that's more than enough. The offense did their part. But did they? They did score six times, three touchdowns and three field goals, but a close look shows the offense didn't look all that great. One of their touchdowns came from that before mentioned fumble recovery in the end zone, and the only reason they were down that far was because of the interception return.

The Cowboys' other two touchdowns came from improv plays where Dak Prescott started to scramble. In fact, on one of those scrambles, he ran into the end zone. When you step back, you see an offense that sputtered quite a bit. They didn't have any creative play calls to free up players. They often didn't have the correct protection set up, giving Cardinals defenders free shots in the backfield. And even when they just needed to get one lousy first down to run out the clock, they couldn't pull it off.

The result was 261 total yards against the defense that gives up the most points in the league. An offense with Prescott, CeeDee Lamb, George Pickens, and several first-round offensive linemen couldn't get the job done in critical moments. As unimpressive as this unit was, they were still outfailed by the defense.

Oh, the defense. What a weird game it was for this group. It's weird because, unlike the offense, they were never meh. They were either great or dreadful. There was no in-between. They started off really sharp. The pressure was there, the coverage was decent, and they were making hard-hitting tackles. The Cardinals couldn't get anything going. To understand this, let's offer some perspective.

With 90 seconds left in the first half, the Cowboys led 14-0. The Cardinals hadn't scored any points yet. With 90 seconds left in the third quarter, the Cowboys led 17-3. The Cardinals only had a field goal through almost three quarters of football. The defense finished with five sacks and two takeaways. They were making plays, but then they went from playing really well to absolutely horrid. What happened?

This is the frustrating part. The Cowboys got comfortable. Instead of playing disciplined, sound football, they wanted to annihilate the Cardinals and got caught overpursuing. On the big running plays, it was a collection of failures. The defensive line didn't maintain their gaps. Instead, they were aggressive and attacked upfield. With holes available, the defensive backs took poor angles and completely whiffed with their tackling. Manageable gains turned into monster plays for the Cardinals.

Those two big running plays allowed Arizona to get back in the game because they were fast strikes. And when the Cowboys offense couldn't finish things on their end, the Cardinals were given more chances to attack, and the defense wasn't equipped to fend them off.

Many will say that Minshew Mania saved the day. That's not what went down. Sure, credit Minshew for making plays, but he wasn't pulling off any Houdini escapes or threading any needles. He just calmly capitalized on coverage breakdowns by the Cowboys' defense. On the Cardinals' final two touchdowns, one to Trey McBride and another to Michael Wilson, there wasn't a defender anywhere close on either play. It was easy, and quite honestly, we all watched helplessly knowing it was coming.

If there's a silver lining, it's that the Cowboys do have talented pieces. Unfortunately, that doesn't mean anything if this team can't find a way to use them correctly. Both the offense and defense are having mental lapses and breakdowns. And it keeps happening week after week with no end in sight.

#2842. Q&A with Hall of Famer Mitch Richmond, talking Yaxel Lendeborg, LeBron, Jokic, Game Pulse

Mitch Richmond is NBA history personified: NBA champion, Rookie of the Year, five-time All-NBA, six-time All-Star and Hall of Famer. For many fans, he is best remembered as part of the legendary Run TMC era of the Golden State Warriors, with Tim Hardaway and Chris Mullin.

Before sitting down for an interview with NBC Sports, Richmond was interacting with fans on the NBA App through Game Pulse. For those unfamiliar, Game Pulse combines a live court visualization with highlights and advanced statistics, including shot charts, in a live-updating experience that also features commentary and perspectives from NBA legends such as Muggsy Bogues, Carlos Boozer, Gary Payton, Joakim Noah and more.Here is our conversation with Richmond (edited for length and clarity).

NBC Sports: You've been on this Game Pulse NBA app experience with the fans. How has that been for you?

Richmond: It's been great. You know, that's my first time being on the Pulse. I always watch the association, and sometimes I already catch everything on NBA TV. But it was my first time, so it was a good experience.

Q: It seems like it's a good experience for the fans. They get real interaction not just with the studio people, but with people like yourself, who happen to be Hall of Famers.

Richmond: Yeah, no question about it. It's just the fan engagement. The fans want to be as close as they can, and sometimes they want to hear from other players watching the game. So it gives me an opportunity to kind of reach out to them, and especially since I played so long ago, so they don't know me. But it's a time to start Googling me. You know what I mean?

Q: I think people underestimate, though, like how great and how impactful Run TMC was, and that era of Warriors basketball. As you look at the league now. Is there a player that kind of reminds you of Tim Hardaway?

Richmond: That's a good question. Oh man, who reminds me of Little Tim? Oh man, I don't, it's hard. He had such power when he dribbles the ball, he gives you that shoulder as he goes by that you can't get back in front of him. Yeah, man, that's hard to do.

Q: It might be even harder with Chris Mullin in some ways.

Richmond: Oh man, let's see who Mully would be. I mean, Mully was not the fastest guy, but it is back to what I say: It's how you use your body, and how you use your skills, and how you move. We see it now in today's game, it's all about getting guys in angles when they pick-and-roll. They want you on their back and they want to make sure that you never get back in front of them. Mully had the tendency, if you get on the side of him, he has a way that you never get back around him. And so he was really, really methodical of how he's going to move and take his little steps, and might do a little bird flip. So yeah, he was very tough to guard.

Q: You're close to the Warriors still to this day. They've been fun to watch at the start of the season. What's impressed you about Yaxel Lendeborg as a rookie, just coming in like this?

Richmond: Coming in with that attitude, man, like I belong. And truly, truly, they need a spark like that. We don't know how long Curry is gonna Curry is gonna give us, but they need a young star to come in and kind of help some of that load for Curry right now. I think he's the guy. He has the temperament, and I like him. I like how he came in with Luka, arguing a little bit, but hey, I think that you need that. You need that spark. You need that spice.

Q: I like that [Warriors coach Steve] Kerr trusted him enough to give him that assignment in his first NBA game.

Richmond: I'm pretty sure he wanted it, and I think that's what great players do at times, man. It's for greatness. I want to guard the best player. You know what I mean. I have no problem. I want to guard the best player. We haven't seen that in a while.

Q: What other rookies have stood out to you?

Richmond: I mean, you look at Caleb. Man, he's going to be a star down in Chicago. Caleb Williams. I like AJ [Dybantsa of the Wizards]. AJ is good. Even the young [Cameron] Boozer. Hopefully, he can start to get his game rolling. But I think it's a lot of good young talent that we have, and I just love watching the games, to tell you the truth.

Q: Houston is off to a fast start. Dallas has knocked off two undefeated teams. What are you liking about those teams? What have you seen from them that's making them look kind of dangerous early?

Richmond: I spent a little time with them in Macau when the Rockets played the Dallas Mavericks over there [preseason], and so they looked like just a fun team. And actually, both teams was personable, and I think they get along with one another. One, and they got two good coaches that's coaching them right now. So, man, I thought Houston had a little bit more than Dallas, but Dallas has been playing excellent this year.

Q: Houston, I like the way they're built. I think they can be really dangerous in a West that feels a little more open right now.

Richmond: No question about it. I think the West is wide open. I think someone... can Portland come out of there? You know Portland is looking pretty good right now. I think the West, like you say, the West is wide open. Lakers are even looking pretty good.

Q: You mentioned Portland. Is there anybody else who's just like, wow! When they're on, I kind of want to watch them. I want to watch those players.

Richmond: I'm loving watching the Knicks, tell you the truth. I love watching the Knicks and how they play. Then I just start going for players to see how they're doing. I go and pop over at Denver and see what [Nikola] Jokic is doing. I'm always on the channel with Golden State. I'm always turning the channel to Dallas. I mean, the Lakers, because the Lakers can actually do something as well. We didn't expect that, but the center that they got from Utah?

Richmond: I mean, no one ever saw this, buried in Utah, and now seeing him, and he was hurt as well. But to see what he's been doing and the numbers he's been putting up, you know, they needed a big man like that to run the floor and catch it at the rim.

Q: On the other side, the Heat have just not gelled yet. It hasn't come together consistently. When you see them, what's missing?

Richmond: I think just a little bit more time together. You know what I mean? I think sometimes, teams mesh very well. Look at Philly. You know, how LeBron [James] just came and and haven't missed the beat over there. They're just playing real fluid ball as well. I think Boston is playing pretty good as well. I think sometimes you need just a little bit more time to gel. We haven't seen that yet, so hopefully they can they can catch a groove and start winning some games.

Q: Philadelphia has gelled really quickly, but is that just LeBron bringing his own culture and that's a team where guys seem willing to sacrifice, and they're kind of in the right place to make plays.

Richmond: I think the sacrifice is a little different when you have a star player coming in that is a volume shooter. LeBron kind of plays every part of the game, so he doesn't need those 40-point nights. You know, he might have one this year, but he has enough guns around him. And I think the guys, you have your shooting hands ready to shoot the ball when you have a guy that is a willing passer, and LeBron has been more of a willing passer, especially when he knows that he got young guns at his beck and call anywhere he goes. These guys can really shoot the ball. Look at Embiid. Embiid is in better shape. The guys are bringing enthusiasm. They had some enthusiasm over there. Let's see if they can keep it the whole year, because at some point in time, teams are going to start figuring them out, and see how it happens. But I do like the start of them; they look like a team to be reckoned with later.

Q: You mentioned Jokic's passing and a couple other guys. There are some great passers in the league right now. Who are some of the guys you want to tune in to watch because they see the court differently?

Richmond: Jokic is that guy. You know, if I played with him, man, I'd be coming off screens all day, going back door because he truly, truly finds guys. We have to put LeBron, he's still there. LeBron still knows how to get passes to the right guys. It's a few others out there, but I just love how guys like that move the ball, share it, and it looks wonderful.

#2839. Angels’ Ken Rosenthal reveals 1st move new owner Stan Kroenke must immediately make

Angels' Ken Rosenthal reveals 1st move new owner Stan Kroenke must immediately make appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Los Angeles Angels have renewed hope heading into the immediate future, as they are under new management after Arte Moreno's disappointing 23-year stint as the owner of the franchise. Earlier this year, Kroenke Sports & Entertainment (KSE) purchased the Angels franchise from Moreno for a staggering $4 billion.

This suggests that a better future is in the cards for the Angels, as the other sports franchises owned by KSE have been vastly successful in their respective leagues. And for MLB insider Ken Rosenthal, this is the first step family patriarch Stan Kroenke must make to kick off the rebuild in Anaheim after they won just 62 games in 2026.

"If I'm Stan Kroenke, my first calls after I am approved as owner of the Los Angeles Angels would be to the Milwaukee Brewers and Tampa Bay Rays. The Brewers, to ask permission to interview Matt Arnold. The Rays, to request the same with Erik Neander," Rosenthal wrote on his latest column for The Athletic.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

"Neither president of baseball operations would necessarily be interested. Both are under contract, so their respective teams might not even allow them to depart. So? The best way for the Angels to send a warning shot to the industry — one even more meaningful than signing free-agent left-hander Tarik Skubal — would be to lure away the architect of one of the World Series participants. Or at the very least, try."

Putting together a contending team starts by having the best minds in the business lead the franchise from the top. This suggestion from Rosenthal, as he so acknowledged, seems a bit ambitious, as the Brewers and Rays have been vastly successful over the past decade or so and aren't just about to let one of the chief minds responsible for their success go without a fight.

Nonetheless, this could be the way the Angels signal their intent to join the other KSE-owned franchises as champions. The Denver Nuggets (in 2023), the Los Angeles Rams (in 2022), the Colorado Avalanche (in 2022), and the English Premier League's Arsenal (in 2026) have all won championships. It's going to be a long road for the Angels, but they have to start somewhere.

#2838. ‘I'm finding my footing': Jaylen Brown's role taking shape with Sixers

'I'm finding my footing': Jaylen Brown's role taking shape with Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Jaylen Brown has spent the opening weeks of the season describing the Sixers as a work in progress.

He offered a similarly humble perspective Monday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena. His performance, however, provided the clearest look yet at how he can influence this group while sharing the floor with the club's other established stars.

Brown finished with 25 points, 11 rebounds and five assists in the Sixers' 126-107 win over the Chicago Bulls. He shot 9 for 15 from the field, made four three-pointers and did not commit a turnover.

Five of his rebounds came on the offensive end, an area Brown has emphasized while the Sixers continue piecing together their frontcourt rotation.

"Just a mindset," Brown said. "Just trying to create extra opportunities for my team. As we're trying to patch together our fives and our front line, I try to make an extra contribution and emphasis on the glass."

Brown has also been careful about how he describes his place on a roster that already belonged to Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey.

Maxey scored 15 of his 26 points in the fourth quarter Monday, continuing his torrid start to the season. He has led the Sixers in scoring while being the only starter to appear in all eight games.

Brown is not interested in interrupting that.

"Maxey's been leading the way, 100 percent," Brown said. "He's been here. This is his team. Him and Joel have been here for a while, so the mindset has been to complement those guys. I'm finding my footing, but Maxey has been leading the way for sure."

Brown's footing looked firm against Chicago.

Beyond his scoring, he helped the Sixers control the glass, moved the ball within an offense that generated 23 assists during the first half and supplied the physicality Nick Nurse expects from him.

"The dude is tough and he wins," Nurse said. "I'll take that all day long. The degree of difficulty on some of the shots he hit was really amazing. I thought he pounded the glass and got some key rebounds. Makes a lot of winning plays. Man, he's a winner."

Brown spent the first 10 years of his career in Boston. He won a championship there, reached another NBA Finals and grew from a 20-year-old prospect into one of the league's premier two-way wings.

Moving into a new system alongside James, Embiid and Maxey was never going to be seamless.

"I mean, he's fitting in along with all of us," James said. "We're getting better and better. It's not going to happen overnight. He spent his first 10 years in a system in Boston, in the city, so it's going to take some adjustment.

"It takes adjustment for him. It's going to take adjustment for the rest of the guys that joined this franchise as well. But tonight was a great step."

James pointed to Brown's "pressure, his shot-making ability, his rebounding ability" and his professionalism.

Brown's early influence has extended beyond his production.

The Sixers entered Monday with one of the NBA's best half-court defenses. Brown has been one of the group's most persistent voices, stressing communication, preparation and attention to opposing players' tendencies.

"That's the part that I care about the most as a group, not individually," Brown said. "Having a mentality night in and night out, respecting your opponent, respecting the game, making sure that we are executing our coverages, we're being clear in our communication and then we're also taking away tendencies on a night-to-night basis.

"Those things I just explained are over 50 percent of the battle."

Brown understands that maintaining those details across an 82-game schedule will require more than reminders.

"A little bit of accountability," he said. "You learn different guys require different things. How you communicate with each guy may be a little different, but accountability for sure and confrontation if need be.

"The offense is going to be what it's going to be, but our defense has got to be non-negotiable. That's something I say all the time. Non-negotiable. You want to be a good team, defense is non-negotiable."

The Sixers showed both sides of their defensive potential Monday.

They built a double-digit lead during the first half, allowed Chicago to score 36 points in the second quarter and then created separation after halftime. Philadelphia held the Bulls to 20 points in the third quarter before pulling away in the fourth.

Brown did not celebrate their ability to raise their intensity after it slipped.

"I don't like that we have that," he said. "It's good that we have shown the potential, but we've got to keep that switch on. It doesn't work like that where you just flip the switch.

"It can catch up to us and it can bite us against a better team or on a night that we're not being as crisp on offense. It can't be a switch just because we're playing a lesser opponent or whatever the case may be. We've got to make sure that mentality is from the beginning to end. Can't skip any steps."

That urgency accompanies considerable optimism.

Brown said the Sixers enjoy playing together. He loves when James grabs a rebound because "he's like a quarterback," capable of launching an outlet pass or immediately pushing the pace.

The group is learning when to attack, when to move the ball and how to avoid hesitating in its effort to play unselfishly.

"It's a lot of talent, man," Brown said. "We like each other, and we're just kind of building that chemistry of how we can get this thing to a well-oiled machine. I think we're taking steps forward every single day."

The Sixers are 7-1 despite Brown believing those steps remain incomplete. They have won close games, erased sizable deficits and received important contributions from different players.

Brown's performance Monday showed another version of how their pieces can fit.

"We still have a long way to go to being a really, really solid, great team," Brown said. "But we've been able to win some games. We started out the season great, 7-1, and you haven't seen our best version yet.

#2836. INTERVIEW: Ex-Dortmund star Patrick Owomoyela on why Jürgen Klopp is "a good fit" for Germany

MÜNCHEN (Bulinews): Ex-Borussia Dortmund star Patrick Owomoyela is confident Jürgen Klopp is the right man who can restore the German national team to it's former glory.

“He is able to change the important things in a short matter of time, and that’s what makes him a good fit," Owomoyela said at a media round in Munich attended by Bulinews.com.

“I wish he could have the Germany guys every day [to practice] but that’s not the case. As I said, he’s able to create an instant impact and a clear vision of what to do.

“Now more than before, his reputation helps players believe what he is saying. All of that is a good fit for a coach with less time but can make things happen.

“It’s just about finding the right people. He used a lot of players [in his first international period] and that will be the case going forward too," the Hamburg native added.

Having played under Klopp at Dortmund for five seasons between 2008 and 2013, Owomoyela gave some fascinating insights into what the 59-year-old demands from his squad.

“He was a coach who pushed for hard work but have fun while doing it. He always had fun it seemed, Even though he asked a lot of us, he would let us have fun as well.

“He was the loudest in the dressing rooms, so he gave you that kind of freedom. But, you had to back it up on the pitch working hard and doing what he asked.

“He could create pictures. He made it really easy to understands where he wanted you to go and what he wanted you to do. It was like a movie in your head.

“So, that kind of ability to get you motivated to do what you need to do, it’s what puts him above many others. He wanted us to play the most intense football.

"It was hunt, hunt, hunt, always hunt. If you got the ball, run fast at your opponent as hard as possible. If they [the opponent] got the ball, go hunt it [back] again.

"He would play games too, if we ran 120 kilometres [as a team] one week, next week he would say let’s do 124 [kilometres]. We believed in what he was saying.

Perhaps the proof is in the pudding. During his time at a once lowly BVB side, he transformed them into a deadly force which claimed two Bundesliga titles and a DFB-Pokal.

Whether that can be replicated on the international stage is up for debate. However, in Owomoyela's mind, Klopp possesses the necessary traits to bring dreams into reality.

#2834. Bo Nix has 15 game-winning drives, most ever for a QB in his first three seasons

The Broncos trailed the Chiefs 24-13 with 10 minutes left on Sunday, but anyone who thought the Broncos were out of it hasn't been watching Bo Nix.

Nix led Denver's offense to two touchdowns and a two-point conversion in the fourth quarter of a 28-24 win, marking Nix's 15th career game-winning drive in the fourth quarter or overtime. No quarterback in NFL history has as many game-winning drives in his first three seasons as Nix.

Dak Prescott, who had 14 game-winning drives in his first three seasons, had the previous record. Nix has surpassed Prescott and isn't even halfway through his third season.

A game-winning drive is defined as an offensive scoring drive in the fourth quarter or overtime that puts a team ahead for the final time. Nix had three of them as a rookie in 2024, an NFL-high seven of them last year, and has an NFL-high five of them already this year.

The all-time leader in game-winning drives is Tom Brady, who had 58 of them. It would take Nix many years to catch Brady, but being more than a quarter of the way there through two and a half seasons is an impressive achievement.

#2832. Bucks Reacts Survey: Are the Milwaukee Bucks too nice?

Welcome to SB Nation Reacts, a survey of fans across the NBA. Throughout the year we ask questions of the most plugged-in Bucks fans and fans across the country.

This Milwaukee Bucks season was never going to be pretty and, so far, well, the Bucks could crack mirrors. There's still plenty to be excited about, of course—namely, the team's three youngest players—but to this point these Bucks aren't exactly the dawgs we hoped they'd be. Porous on the perimeter and feeble inside, are these Bucks simply too nice?

After seven games, the Bucks have shown promise on the glass—even if there are some warning signs—in an attempt to win the possession game and maximise their opportunities to keep the scoreboard ticking over and the games close. But there is more than one way to skin a cat, and the Bucks aren't doing much to make life difficult for their opponents—certainly not from a disruption point of view. So far, the Bucks sit dead last in steals per game (5.6 SPG), nearly half a steal worse than their nearest opponent (the Memphis Grizzlies, who average 6.0 SPG). They're also dead last in opponent turnovers, forcing just 12.7 per game (nearly a full turnover fewer than the New York Knicks and Utah Jazz, who force 13.6 turnovers a night). All this means that Milwaukee isn't generating extra looks through its defence.

Compounding the issue, the Bucks are mediocre at best when it comes to turning back shot attempts. Myles Turner and (sometimes) Kel'el Ware aside, these Bucks simply do not block shots, averaging just 4.6 BPG thus far, "good" for 18th in the league. But the Bucks' rim troubles don't stop there. They're currently 23rd in the league in defending the paint, conceding 50.9 points in the paint per night, while also allowing 27.6 free throw attempts per night (22nd in the league). It should come as no surprise then that they rank 21st in the league in opponent effective field goal percentage (at 54.8%).

Realistically, these numbers shouldn't be too surprising. While Turner blocks shots, he's never been an elite defender or physical presence—and he's the Buck with the best defensive reputation. Kyle Kuzma and Jaime Jaquez Jr. compete, but they don't have the physical gifts or explosive athleticism to be more than useful team defenders. In the backcourt, Tyler Herro has long been a defensive liability—or at least regarded as such. And none of these guys have any "nasty" in 'em.

Ryan Rollins, on the other hand, has the length, and has shown the capacity to lock down at times. He's also got some of that defensive stink to him (see his battles with Tyrese Haliburton). But in the early season, Rollins has seemed more intent on getting his shot off in a completely understandable bid to (1) prove himself worthy of the expectations placed on him prior to the season and (2) prove himself worthy of a new, lucrative contract—from Milwaukee or someone else—next offseason. Forest for the trees type stuff.

Of course, it's also worth noting that, despite their limitations, the starting group prior to last night's change-up has actually been a real positive for the Bucks. This group—the team's most frequent five-man lineup with 75 minutes of playing time—has a +21.6 net rating (per Basketball-Reference). Comparatively, just four of the team's 19 other five-man lineups have fared better, but each of these have played fewer than seven minutes together. In fact, besides the starting group, no other five-man lineup has played more than 17 minutes of action thus far, making it difficult to truly determine which combinations are working and which aren't.

Another factor to consider is the team's bench. Outside of AJ Green, the core reserves are all very young, meaning they're simultaneously learning the NBA game and trying to establish themselves in the league. This is not a new combination, but it's not an easy one either—and certainly not one that typically leads to winning basketball. Youth aside, the Bucks' bench also lacks any true defensive aces—at least at this stage—despite their effort (see Kasparas Jakučionis, Brayden Burries, and Nate Ament) or gifts (see Kel'el Ware). All of this is to say that, like the players themselves, Jon Horst has more work to do.

On a bigger scale, could the Bucks' struggles to disrupt opposing offences in part boil down to a mantra malfunction? Their new motto, "Compete. Together. Get better," sure is wholesome, but is it all a bit too Brady Bunch? And is Jenkins leaning too much into relationship-building with his new herd of fawns? His decision to bench Rollins suggests not. The likely answer, then, is that this is simply a case of culture first, results later. Build rapport—nurture the youth—then let the metamorphoses begin.

Regardless, for the Bucks to start doing more than show glimpses between the cracks, they might have to find a way to get a little meaner.

What do you think? Are these Bucks too squeaky clean? Let us know your thoughts by voting below—and don't forget to explain your reasons in the comments.

#2830. Will Jonathan Taylor, Brian Thomas Jr. get traded? How will Raiders decide on future with Maxx Crosby? What league sources are saying

With just one week left until the 2026 NFL trade deadline on Nov. 10, texts and calls are bouncing from front office to front office.

Multiple landscape-shifting trends have already materialized. The Chicago Bears' trade of two first-round draft picks for defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons is the richest and perhaps highest-ceiling move. The New York Giants' fifth-round pick for now-starting quarterback J.J. McCarthy may net the best value.

Since the season's start, the Houston Texans have traded for receiver Marvin Harrison Jr., the Dallas Cowboys have traded for cornerback Joey Porter Jr., the Seattle Seahawks have traded for left guard Quenton Nelson and the San Francisco 49ers have traded for defensive tackle DeForest Buckner.

Yahoo Sports spoke with NFL general managers, salary-cap executives, personnel evaluators and more to take a pulse of the trade deadline landscape. All were granted anonymity due to the competitive implications of the intel. The summary: Expect more moves during the next week. But also consider the average impact of most moves (we're not looking at you, Bears) more conservatively than initial hype may dictate.

"Usually if you're looking at this time of year, a real need, you're probably not filling it," a high-ranking NFC executive said by phone Tuesday. "You might be solidifying it. I call it, patching a leak. … You might not have planned for the Colts to be in a spiral and now their guard who is going to be a UFA, but he comes available.

"Relax and just see if there's any opportunities."

An executive from a second NFC team explained they're "always willing to make moves … if the price is right." But how often is it?

"Unless you're paying up for this stud, do any of these deals really make sense when prices are kind of flying all over the place?" the executive told Yahoo Sports. "That, to me, is never really where the best deals get made. … But if there's a [deal] that's available that feels too good to be true or the other way around, you get an offer you can't refuse?

"I think obviously you have to come into that with eyes wide open."

It's easy to draw parallels between Jacksonville Jaguars receiver Brian Thomas Jr. and Houston Texans receiver Marvin Harrison Jr. That begs the question: Will any team pay for Thomas the second-round draft pick (slightly discounted with a sixth-for-seventh-round swap) that the Texans paid the Arizona Cardinals for Harrison?

The Jaguars selected Thomas 23rd overall in the 2024 NFL Draft, 19 picks after the Cardinals selected Harrison the same year. Thomas' career peak thus far was higher, with 87 catches for 1,282 yards and 10 touchdowns as a rookie to Harrison's 62 catches for 885 yards and eight touchdowns the same year. Each player's production and catch percentage have fallen since, and Houston banked on a reunion with Harrison's college quarterback, C.J. Stroud, reviving him. Will a fresh start revive Thomas?

"Sometimes we'll scout a player and we'll say, 'This guy is going to be better for the second team he plays for than the team that originally [drafts him] just because they kind of need a change of scenery, a wake-up call," one AFC personnel evaluator said. "'Hey, it didn't work out and your career's about to end' — that kind of pressure.

"He definitely would fall in that category, in my opinion."

At 6-foot-2 7/8, per his scouting combine measurement, with a 4.33 40-yard dash, Thomas' size and speed allures talent evaluators. His catch reliability concerns them, Thomas falling from a 65.4% catch rate per target as a rookie to 52.7% last year and 45.5% this year. Thomas has also registered 19 drops, including 10 last year, per Pro Football Reference.

From the Kansas City Chiefs to the Pittsburgh Steelers, multiple teams on track to make the playoffs need a receiver. The Washington Commanders pique the interest of some around the league as a Harrison/Stroud-like fit that would reunite teammates and friends from Daniels' LSU Heisman days. Others wonder if Thomas' inconsistency would hurt Daniels' ability to operate a clean offense more than it'd help give the Commanders another target after Terry McLaurin's season-ending patellar tear. One high-ranking executive noted that a timing and precision offense like Liam Coen's in Jacksonville is unlikely to best serve Thomas as a target.

A third-round pick seems roughly what the league expects Thomas to cost, an executive from one NFC team comparing his value to that of George Pickens heading from the Steelers to the Dallas Cowboys in May 2025 for a third-round pick (with a sixth/fifth swap), while an executive from another NFC team thought the Jaguars would need to wait until the pressure of deadline day to command a third rather than a fourth. A high-ranking AFC executive noted that the Jaguars had previously shopped Thomas but earlier this season said they were no longer interested in moving him.

If the Jaguars stay put with Thomas, what will explain clinging to a player with 10 catches for 117 yards and two touchdowns in seven games? Targets aren't Thomas' only value, as the Jaguars use him to create space for fellow receivers like Parker Washington and Jakobi Meyers.

"If you trade him and then now … don't have speed, so now the defenses play more compact, it's just harder," a high-ranking NFC executive said. "It's less space. So with that being said, a third may not be enough. It's like, 'Hey, we're actually a good team. Why would we give this guy up for a third?'"

This question has loomed over the trade deadline since, and really even before, the Baltimore Ravens agreed to send the Las Vegas Raiders two first-round picks for Crosby in March. They infamously reneged after Crosby arrived in Baltimore and took a physical that underscored the degeneration in his knee.

The overwhelming belief around the league since: Crosby does not want to be traded. And when the Raiders opened their season 3-0, giving Crosby the main missing piece of his Raiders experience (wins), two high-ranking AFC executives agreed: Trading Crosby no longer made sense. That sentiment continued to capture conversations with executives while the Raiders were 3-1 and 3-2 before shifting slightly as the Raiders fell below (and then climbed back to) .500. So where do things stand with Crosby, who's recorded three sacks, one forced fumble, one fumble recovery and 18 quarterback hits in eight games?

"They've already traded him once," a high-ranking NFC executive said, "so I'm sure that they would listen."

Sources around the league believe that for an offer like the two first-round picks Baltimore presented, or value equivalent to a first- and second-round pick, the Raiders would pick up the phone. But the sense is not that the Raiders are actively trying to offload Crosby, who provides them value now as well as whenever they transition from Kirk Cousins to rookie first overall pick Fernando Mendoza at quarterback. Executives painted a picture of what they think the Raiders may be considering: On one hand, assistant general manager Brian Stark came from the Denver Broncos, fresh off seeing how Denver's strong defense helped Bo Nix develop as a rookie. Could that sway his belief on what Mendoza needs? On the other hand, first-year head coach Klint Kubiak is the team's offensive play-caller. The more capital to collect elite linemen and skill players to run his zone scheme, the more he'll be able to execute his offensive vision.

"He's probably more valuable to keep, but if you can get two ones for him, you should flip him," an NFC executive said. "I'd probably just hang on to him because you've shown that you're already better than you thought you were… [and] then let's say Mendoza comes on at the end of the year and looks good?

"You're better off having him in 2027 and 2028 because there's a chance you can make a push at that point."

Jonathan Taylor is the most valuable skill position player in the deadline mix. That doesn't guarantee he'll be traded.

League sources stress that equating the Indianapolis Colts' trade of Nelson and Buckner with a trade of Taylor is disingenuous: Nelson and Buckner were each on expiring contracts and likely to leave this spring in free agency. Taylor signed a two-year, $44 million extension with $39 million guaranteed nearly three months ago. He's 27 years old to Nelson and Buckner's 30 and 32, respectively. And he could be a building block for a new regime that already fired general manager Chris Ballard and seems to view head coach Shane Steichen's long-term future with serious questions.

Executives from multiple teams believe Taylor should command something similar to what the 49ers paid for Christian McCaffrey. On Oct. 20, 2022, the 49ers sent the Carolina Panthers 2023 second-, third- and fourth-round picks as well as a 2024 fifth-round pick in exchange for McCaffrey, who has since earned All-Pro honors in both of his healthy seasons.

Two caveats suggest the price could be a little lighter for Taylor than McCaffrey. First, McCaffrey's heavier usage in the passing game offsets concerns about the depreciating value of aging running backs in ways that Taylor's style might not. Secondly, the Niners were bidding against their division-rival Los Angeles Rams for McCaffrey in 2022: That auction drove the price up. Will anyone pay the Colts what it takes? Some league sources point to the Minnesota Vikings as a team that makes sense. Their defense ranks first in points allowed and second in yardage, playing like a true Super Bowl-contending unit. Their offense, meanwhile, is struggling at 27th in points scored and 29th in yards gained. The Seattle Seahawks have raised eyebrows too, though the Seahawks just showed a disinterest in paying big for Kenneth Walker III this spring. Notably, first-year Vikings general manager Nolan Teasley was part of the Seattle group that let Walker walk.

"He's a rare exception of somebody like a running back who makes such a difference that you just ... whatever they want, you just pay it because they have potential to make a run this year if they get a guy like him," an NFC executive said of the Vikings-Taylor potential. "If they don't, they're going to stall out. They know that because they know their passing game isn't good enough right now. …

"While you got the bull by the horns, you should try to be aggressive to go, at least make sure you get to the divisional round in the playoffs."

An AFC executive underscored why the Colts should trade Taylor as well as receiver Josh Downs, rather than taking picks for only Buckner and Nelson.

"Teams that do the best job on these trades is really understanding what phase you're in and stacking," the executive said. "All of those picks combined make an intriguing rebuild. One extra third-round pick doesn't really help you."

No one's suggesting that the current iteration of Eagles running back Saquon Barkley nor Titans running back Tony Pollard can elevate a team like Taylor can. Each would be cheaper than Taylor accordingly, but Barkley no longer makes sense for a trade. League sources weren't surprised to hear Barkley say on Sunday night that he's been told he won't be traded. While overwhelming league sentiment is that the Eagles should aim to recoup value, and especially salary cap space from their roster, amid their injury-riddled, lost season, Barkley's hamstring and back injuries tank his value likely beyond the point of a helpful return. Before his injuries worsened, executives pegged Barkley's value with his contract as ranging from a second to a fourth-round pick.

Hurting the Eagles' case is that while some positions last well from season to season, the league's view on running-back depreciation tends to limit running-back trades to teams wanting to maximize their acquisition this year. Barkley's limitations hamper that hope.

"That is one position the draft always looks better because age is such a negative thing," an AFC talent evaluator said. "And I'd say running back or skill positions like receiver, usually the need is immediate so they don't compare. If someone acquired Saquon or [Taylor], I think it'll be for this season more than anything."

The Eagles still could shop players. Defensive tackle Moro Ojomo, tight end Dallas Goedert and offensive lineman Tyler Steen are all in the last year of their contracts. Ojomo drew high-value interest before the season, per a league source, but the Eagles declined. With their 2026 season spiraling, would they reconsider sending Ojomo away for something like a second-round pick?

"I think they would probably take the second now," a high-ranking executive from another NFC team said. "But probably going into the year, they'd be like, OK, if we're winning, you're at least planning on trying to win. And then you're like, 'Well, we're not.'"

To round out the running back conversation, Pollard is a cost-effective, healthy and still potentially valuable back who could give a team solid depth. His 595 yards and six touchdowns rushing this season each rank ninth in the league, and Tennessee could seek value for a player likely to walk this spring anyway. A team renting Pollard for the second half of the season might need to pay a fourth or slightly more to award the Titans more than the compensatory pick they'd otherwise get.

League wisdom says the last winless team, the Los Angeles Chargers, should trade players before the deadline to amass the capital needed to rebound from their 0-7 start. But who would this franchise trade? Edge rusher Tuli Tuipulotu is in the final year of his rookie deal, following up his 2025 13-sack season with an interception, two forced fumbles and a fumble recovery in seven games so far. He also leads all edge rushers in run-stop win rate, per ESPN's metric. Executives from multiple teams think he'd draw value akin to a second-round draft pick even with just half a year left on his deal. But trading away young edge rushers is a risky proposition, and the Chargers are "a relatively conservative bunch," as one high-ranking NFC executive said. Executives and evaluators believe it would make little sense, however, for the Chargers not to offload 35-year-old Khalil Mack. He's also up after this season and regardless of the pick back, trading the remainder of Mack's $18 million salary is worth it, they say. Mack could help a team needing pass rush, but at this stage of his career, "I wouldn't count on him as a game-changer," the high-ranking NFC executive said.

Falcons safety Jessie Bates III could be a win-win option for a contending team. The Falcons drafted safety Xavier Watts in the third round last year and safety Billy Bowman Jr. in the fourth last year before trading for Sydney Brown this spring. Atlanta's depth at the position, paired with their 3-5 record and quarterback questions, should help the Falcons rationalize trading away an impact defender. In eight games, Bates has 46 tackles, three forced fumbles, one fumble recovery touchdown and one interception. He could immediately elevate a contender that's currently stronger on offense than defense. One NFC executive pointed to the Buffalo Bills as a fit to narrow the gap between their powerhouse offense and shaky defense, while an executive from another NFC team thought the Bills would be more interested in pass-rushing help. (The Bills released safety C.J. Gardner-Johnson on Tuesday, opening another potential case for Bates.) Executives pegged the likely price to rent Bates at a third-round pick, with the possibility to creep to a second.

Executives across the league remain split on whether they'd have traded two first-round picks for Jeffery Simmons, who starred in his Monday night debut with the Chicago Bears. Simmons' six tackles, one sack, two tackles for loss and pass deflection wreaked havoc on the Seahawks in a 34-17 Bears win. Some executives believe the price was too steep, pegging Simmons' value as closer to a first- and second-round pick or "one and change." The Bears became the first team in the modern draft era to spend two first-round picks on an interior defensive lineman. But one high-ranking NFC executive said they respect the Bears recognizing their window and chasing it. A talent evaluator from another NFC team explained why they're more comfortable trading for a 29-year-old defensive tackle than a speed rusher.  "As a speed rusher, there is going to be a level of depreciation that you're going to expect from this player regardless … " the evaluator said. "These large prototype, explosive, powerful defensive linemen I would say do tend to have a level of normal regression that I think can protect your investment."

#2829. Corinthians celebrate first Brasileirão Under-17 title

Corinthians won the 2026 Brazilian U-17 ChampionshipCredits: Rafael Ribeiro/CBF

Until this Tuesday (3), the Brazilian U-17 Championship trophy was still missing from Corinthians' trophy cabinet. Not anymore. Despite a 3–1 defeat in Belo Horizonte, Timão secured the title thanks to their 3–0 win in São Paulo, winning 4–3 on aggregate.

For Erasmo Damiani, Corinthians' youth football executive, winning the Brazilian U-17 Championship reflects the efforts of the players and coaching staff throughout the season.

"This achievement is incredibly inspiring. The coaching staff gave their all to help the players perform. It's an undeniable achievement, because it's our first national youth league title. It's something much more special for everyone," Damiani celebrated, referring to the club's lack of titles in the Brazilian U-17 and U-20 Championships—Corinthians won the U-17 Copa do Brasil in 2016.

The São Paulo side's head coach, Guilherme Nascimento, explained that the title was the result of a process of connecting the players with the club's "spirit and history."

"This title means so much. We worked hard with them on the pride and identity that come with wearing this shirt. Whenever Corinthians enters a competition, we need to be at the top. Today we managed to put Corinthians back at the top of Brazilian youth football. This is very important in everyone's lives, and it will be a milestone in the careers of these players, who will go on to achieve so much more. This achievement is hugely significant for their development and for our work as a whole," he said.

Corinthians won the 2026 Brazilian U-17 ChampionshipCredits: Rafael Ribeiro/CBF

Full-back Pedro Fernandes was instrumental in securing the title. With Corinthians under pressure from Cruzeiro's attacks and trailing 3–0, the defender tried his luck from outside the box. Goalkeeper Diogo parried his shot onto the crossbar, sending the ball into the air before it fell, struck Diogo himself and ended up in the back of the net.

With a mixture of courage and luck behind the goal, he stressed that, even before the match began, he had felt he would play a decisive role in Corinthians' triumph.

"It's very rewarding. I prayed to God so much for this. Before the match, I told my teammates that I was going to decide the title and that the goal would be mine. And sure enough, God blessed me with this goal," he celebrated.

#2827. 'We don't have enough time to recover:' Is the NBA game outpacing the players?

Standing near half-court with a towel draped over his shoulders, Thunder center Isaiah Hartenstein attempted to drum up encouragement for his rookie deputy Aday Mara as he approached the bench. Teammates Micah Peavy and Bennett Stirtz waited to do the same, while occasionally tilting their heads back at the jumbotron, which flashed a deficit of nearly 20 points. Veteran guard Alex Caruso, dressed in street clothes, could only clap his hands in hopes of bringing a jolt of energy that simply would not come in a 119-98 loss to the Rockets — an unimaginable, disheartening new normal.

The 2026-27 season kicked off a fortnight ago, but Oklahoma City has already endured a campaign's worth of misfortune. Jalen Williams, lost to a fractured left fibula and a dislocated ankle. Cason Wallace, lost to a torn left labrum. Chet Holmgren, lost to a right patellar tendon tear. A tight-knit group that has always preached the importance of being where their feet are now is struggling to find its footing. A team that stood atop basketball's pinnacle less than 18 months ago now is dealing with questions about ever seriously challenging again, despite still boasting the reigning MVP and a former Coach of the Year. It's a unique type of hoops purgatory the organization hasn't experienced before.

"The morale's been pretty good all things considered," coach Mark Daigneault said on Saturday. "A week ago, the circumstances were a lot different than they are today. We understand that. We try to have an approach that can transcend these circumstances and the guys have done a great job. Obviously, the challenge is a little different now than it was prior to those injuries, but we have to recalibrate to what the challenge is and attack it a day and a game at a time."

While injuries have always been a part of the game, especially when competing at the highest level of the sport, the widespread volume and degree of ailments is troubling. One team being suddenly down 60% of its projected starting lineup is one thing, albeit a needle-in-a-haystack type probability. But the list of unavailables — Anthony Edwards, Tyrese Haliburton, Ja Morant, Ben Simmons, Jaren Jackson Jr., Paolo Banchero, Jalen Duren, Kawhi Leonard, Jalen Suggs and others — is growing, with various timelines ranging from multiple weeks to multiple months. Stars and teams are also being cautious with back-to-backs, whether due to early load management or having returned from long-term injuries. (The Pacers had 10 players out against the Hawks on Monday night, the second night of a back-to-back, citing a combination of rest and injury management.)

But the games aren't slowing down for anyone. The season has become streamlined to some degree, with the shortening of training camp/preseason (the typical ramp-up period) and the insertion of the NBA Cup. It's clearly taken a toll on the players, which in turn takes a toll on the overall product. And there doesn't seem to be an immediate fix for cramming 82 games into a six-month schedule.

Rockets guard Marcus Smart, who's dealt with serious injuries over his 12-year career, had an honest take on the growing epidemic.

"The level of basketball has become so extreme on the human body and we don't have enough time to recover like we used to," Smart said. "When I first came in the league, training camp and preseason were damn near three weeks, multiple games to get your body ready for an 82-game season. Now, depending on how far you go in the playoffs, you might get two months off? It's tough on the body.

"People don't understand; you're flying, your body's inflamed, you're landing in different time zones, go to sleep late and gotta be up early. Run as hard as you can, jump as high and fast as you can and be as physical as you can. All you're doing is asking yourself for injuries. But we signed up for it, and have to figure out a way to minimize it. Some of our best players are getting hurt and it's very unfortunate. We all want to be out here doing what we love."

There is a long list of sports scientists, doctors, data analysts and professionals who will attempt to dissect such a rapid rise of injuries in a short amount of time. But simply watching every night, the games look more rigorous, more demanding and more taxing.

Possessions are up, with the Chicago Bulls topping out at 110.2 a night, up from 107 courtesy of the 2025-26 Miami Heat. League average pace from the previous campaign is now hovering over the bottom third. The game has also become more aggressive. Last season's Pistons team, thought to be one of the more physical units around, committed 22 fouls per game. That same figure would rank 18th today. More teams are comfortable sending bodies to the offensive glass, with the Milwaukee Bucks' 33 chances per night a whopping 10 marks higher than last season's No. 1. On the defensive end, no team averaged double digits in contested rebound rate last season — there are eight such teams now. Average team speed and distance are up, too.

"There's not just one straightforward answer," said Jeff Stotts, injury analyst and owner of InStreetClothes.com. "I think you're seeing younger players get injured with a higher frequency because they're likely to have more miles on their legs before they get to the NBA. … I think the game has outpaced the player. And now you're asking your bigger, stronger athletes to also cover more ground, not only work on accelerators but also their brakes. Asking our big guys to stretch their games all the way out to the 3-point line. All those things increase the chances and likelihood of injuries occurring along the way."

Last season, only three players averaged more than 36 minutes a night — Amen Thompson, Kevin Durant and Luka Dončić. That list has since nearly tripled in size. The aforementioned list of injured stars sans Haliburton also has another thing in common: all of their usage rates were lower than the previous season. What that means is while you may be on the ball less, you're off the ball more — relocating, cutting, changing direction and using your body in other ways that still can accumulate pressure and load.

A good indicator of injury prevention is measuring an athlete's workload against their overall fitness level. Essentially the more they're asked to do physically, the more time they need to recover.

"We're in this constant back and forth between rest and acute-to-chronic load ratio, where we're trying to minimize spikes," Stotts said. "Sudden increases in load on the body in relationship to the overall conditioning of the athlete. The teams that have found success have found ways to quantify the data in a way that helps buy-in. Some teams won't rest players; others will build it in. There's no great cure."

Is such a spike in absences this early into the season enough to warrant an alternative look at scheduling or the larger umbrella of how the sport is played altogether? No magic wand can guarantee a clean bill of health, but the games, pace and aggression are increasing in frequency — and the players, whom the entire business model and ecosystem revolves around, are suffering because of it.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2826. Penn State notes: WR Chase Sowell is on track to play at Washington

Penn State probably will have at least one injured wide receiver on the trip later this week to Washington, coach Matt Campbell said Tuesday.

Chase Sowell, the Nittany Lions' leading receiver, missed last week's 31-17 win over Purdue with a leg injury. Koby Howard, who averaged almost 29 yards a catch in the first three games, has missed the last five games, also with a leg injury.

"The word 'probable' is what I would use with Chase," Campbell said. "I think he's in a really good spot. I think Koby is still probably in the word 'questionable,' and I think we'll have to see where he's at.

"It's the same rhetoric that I guess I've said the last couple weeks. When he's ready, we're going to try to make sure he's on the field, but we're going to make sure he's ready to go."

Sowell, a senior, has 34 receptions for 510 yards and three touchdowns. A sophomore, Howard has 12 catches for 344 yards and two TDs.

Without Sowell and Howard, Penn State tight ends Andrew Rappleyea, Ben Brahmer and Gabe Burkle combined for nine catches for 115 yards and one TD against Purdue.

"You have to find a way to create explosive plays," offensive coordinator Taylor Mouser said. "When you don't have some of your most explosive athletes, it makes things hard. We always cater to the personnel that we have, whether it's the running backs, the tight ends or the other wide receivers."

Penn State cornerback Audavion Collins picked up the first carry of his career when he rushed for 11 yards in the fourth quarter against Purdue.

"We didn't have a ton of guys who could be like AC," Mouser said. "I've been thinking about it for a long time. …He's one of the faster guys that we have. It was good to see him go out there and create an explosive play for us."

Collins is in his second season as a starter for the Lions after he transferred from Mississippi State following the 2022 season. He leads Penn State with nine pass breakups.

"I just love what that kid's about," Mouser said. "He just loves football. He's tenacious."

Penn State backup quarterback Alex Manske hasn't taken a snap in Big Ten play and probably won't the rest of the year unless one of the four remaining games gets out of hand one way or the other.

Campbell was asked Tuesday whether he will try to get him snaps to prepare for next season when starter Rocco Becht's eligibility will have expired.

"It's a really fair question," Campbell said. "When the lights come on and you get the opportunity to play, what does it look like? Whether we get those opportunities to foster that, that'll be great. That is not my intent.

"My intent is to have this team playing its best football down the stretch, and that continues to be our priority. But if those opportunities would ever exist, we're going to do everything in our power to try to get Alex in the football game."

Penn State's traveling party for the Washington game Saturday at 3:30 p.m. (TV-FS1) will ride on buses Thursday from State College to Harrisburg International Airport, where it will take a direct flight to Seattle.

The last two years, the Lions also traveled on Thursday to Los Angeles for games against USC in 2024 and UCLA in 2025.

"The time of the game was really big," Campbell said when asked why they're leaving Thursday. "I guess it's a 12:35 start time there. I felt like if we got in Friday, boy, you're going to be really pushing it.

"In our past (at Iowa State), we've had to go to the West Coast. If it was a day game, we tried to go on Thursday to get ourselves there, get settled in and try to, from a sleep standpoint, get on the best rhythm that we possibly can and not lose sleep."

#2824. Bills release player suspended for spitting on Raiders tight end

The Buffalo Bills have released safety C.J. Gardner-Johnson, the team announced on Nov. 3.

The move comes just one day after Gardner-Johnson finished serving a one-game suspension for spitting at an opposing player. Prior to the suspension, the 28-year-old had started all six games for Buffalo after signing a deal in free agency with the Bills over the offseason.

Gardner-Johnson was ejected before halftime in Buffalo's Week 6 win for spitting at Las Vegas Raiders tight end Brock Bowers. The safety posted an apology to his Instagram story after the incident:

"I want to take a moment to address my actions and sincerely apologize to my opponent, my teammates, my organization, and everyone who supports me," Gardner-Johnson wrote. "My passion and competitive nature have always been a huge part of who I am, but I also understand that there is a line that should never be crossed. The gesture I made toward my opponent was inappropriate, unnecessary, and not a reflection of the respect I have for him or this game.

"There is no excuse for my actions, and I take full accountability. No matter how emotional or intense the competition gets, I have to hold myself to a higher standard."

Kalen DeBoer's Crimson Tide lost consecutive games to Georgia and Tennessee in October. However, wins over top-25 foes Kentucky and South Carolina apparently offset the losses along with the seventh most difficult schedule in the FBS, according to ESPN Analytics.

RELATED: Why Alabama is ranked ahead of Tennessee

"When you're trying to compare teams head to head, they have to be in the same pod to be compared head to head," CFP chair Hunter Yurachek said. "Alabama and Kentucky were in the same pod, they were compared because of their head-to-head matchup. Alabama got the nod ahead of Kentucky, because of that."

The ESPN analysts and social media didn't really agree.

"Doesn't the committee realize Nick Saban is not at Bama anymore," Danny Kanell posted on X.

#2820. Chicago Bears week 9 power rankings roundup: Among the best

With an emphatic win Monday night against the Seattle Seahawks, the Chicago Bears have launched into the Super Bowl contender conversation.

This team is for real. They have a great quarterback and coach. Their defense has been elevated with the addition of Jeffery Simmons.

The team has fought the regression narrative. They've fought the "yeah but…" caveats that came with their wins and the "but what about…" that came with the losses.

Now they have to work against the hype and the target on their back.

How high are they in this week's rankings?

Defense was a need going into the offseason, and it remained a need heading into the season. Despite playing better than expected, it made sense that Chicago was still an impact piece (or two) away from being considered a legitimate Super Bowl contender. Even after adding Jeffery Simmons, additional defensive line help or a starting-caliber cornerback should still be a priority. Another sneaky need: tight end depth, following the season-ending injury to Cole Kmet.

The Athletic – Chad Graff and Josh Kendall

Reality check: The bull's-eye is on them now

The addition of Jeffery Simmons paid immediate dividends for the Bears defense. The new defensive tackle, acquired from Tennessee for two first-round picks, had six tackles and a sack on Monday night. Caleb Williams became the first quarterback this season to throw for more than 200 yards against the Seahawks, and the offense put up the most overall yards (363) of anyone this season against Seattle. The Bears are officially contenders.

Biggest need: Still defense, though the big move was made

The Bears' front office understood the assignment. Chicago traded two first-round draft picks to Tennessee for defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons. If there's a bigger move at the deadline from any team, it'll be a surprise. Simmons is exactly what the Bears' defense needed as a disruptor on the line, and he notched a sack in his Chicago debut on Monday night against Seattle. Chicago clearly feels like it's a contender now and was willing to sacrifice some high future draft picks for a shot at making a deep playoff run.

That doesn't mean the Bears should be done on defense, though any other move wouldn't be major. A mid-round draft pick for pass rushing help would continue to improve the defensive front. A reunion with Khalil Mack would make a ton of sense. But make no mistake: The Bears already made their all-in move.

"Super Bowl teams all have what we call a 'red-level' interior defensive lineman," Bears general manager Ryan Poles said of the Simmons trade, via the Chicago Sun-Times. "You need one.

"We've been searching, but sometimes the draft doesn't provide that to you. Free agency doesn't really [provide them]; it's very hard for guys to get out of the building when they have all of those things that we're talking about. [Simmons] was an opportunity that we had to jump on."

3. Chicago Bears (6) Those who were familiar with Jeffery Simmons' game knew he'd make an immediate difference for a Chicago front that needed an elite disruptor. The rest of the football world quickly learned about him in Monday night's 34-17 road win over the defending champion Seahawks. Simmons elevated the Bears' front by multiple levels, pressuring Sam Darnold and eventually attracting double teams that freed Montez Sweat for more one-on-one rushes in what doubled as a preview of the conundrum every team will have to navigate going forward. Chicago isn't just an offensive machine anymore; this is a team with a defense that just might be able to complement Caleb Williams and Co.

4. Chicago Bears (8) They went into Seattle and beat up the Seahawks. The Jeffrey Simmons trade paid off right away with a dominant first game.

2. Chicago Bears (2): QB Caleb Williams and newly acquired DT Jeffery Simmons certainly sowed fresh hope in front of a national audience on Monday night that Chicago might get its first NFL title in four decades after Da new-look Bears knocked off the reigning Super Bowl champion Seahawks. In Seattle. In prime time. Quite a statement win.

Week 8 result: Beat the Seahawks 34-17Week 8 ranking: 7

Best in-season trade acquisition ever: DE Montez Sweat in 2023

Three seasons before the Bears sent three draft picks, including two first-rounders, to Tennessee to acquire All-Pro defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons, GM Ryan Poles got aggressive in 2023 to land Sweat. Chicago sent a 2024 second-rounder to Washington in exchange for Sweat, who led both the Commanders (6.5) and Bears (6.0) in sacks in 2023, making him the first player in NFL history to lead two different teams in sacks in the same season. Sweat recorded his first double-digit-sack season and reached similar production in 2025, when he led Chicago with 10 sacks. Coach Ben Johnson called that season the best of Sweat's career. — Courtney Cronin

Bears (No. 8; 6-2): The Bears are NOT who we thought they were — they're serious contenders to win the whole thing.

What do you think of this week's rankings? Let us know in the comments.

#2819. Adou Thiero ‘trending in the right direction,’ could return from knee injury by end of week

After garnering considerable praise during the offseason and training camp for his development and diligent work, Adou Thiero has unfortunately yet to suit up in the regular season as he deals with swelling in his left knee. But according to head coach JJ Redick, it sounds like the second-year forward is potentially nearing a return.

"He's had some live exposures and I think he's trending in the right direction," Redick told reporters prior to the Los Angeles Lakers' win over the Miami Heat on Monday. "Hopefully by the end of the week he'll be available."

Thiero's latest injury bout marks a turbulent first year and change in the NBA. He missed considerable time as a rookie with swelling in his left knee, which sidelined him for all of preseason and the first 13 games of the regular season, before a sprained MCL in his right knee rendered him unavailable from late December to mid-February.

Fortunately, it sounds like he should be back sooner rather than later; although, swelling in the same knee a year later is undoubtedly concerning.

His point-of-attack defense and general activity, both of which popped at times during the preseason, could help invigorate the Lakers' 18th-ranked defense. They've often struggled to contain the ball and looked overmatched athletically on the perimeter at times. If he can find a reliable role offensively — and playing alongside Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves increases that possibility — Thiero's size and athleticism can remedy some of those issues.

For now, though, the focus is on Thiero becoming fully healthy and returning to the floor. Once he does that, determining whether there's a spot for him in Los Angeles' wing rotation, one that's off to a pretty commendable start through eight games, can enter the conversation.

In the meantime, Los Angeles will keep relying on Ziaire Williams, Matisse Thybulle, Jake LaRavia and Quentin Grimes to hold things down at the forward spots. The Lakers will look for their seventh win of the season when they face the Sacramento Kings inside Golden 1 Center Wednesday night at 7 p.m.

#2817. Instant Analysis: Blues Search For Three-Game Winning Streak Bungled Again In 3-1 Loss To Rangers

You knew what was at stake, a chance at three straight wins. We all know how this script plays out.

And true to form for the St. Louis Blues, it played out just was predicted: a loss.

The Blues' 3-1 loss to the New York Rangers at Madison Square Garden in New York on Monday feels like another missed opportunity for a team looking for consistency.

Joel Hofer gave the Blues (7-7-1) a chance to win and kept them in it, but they were less than a minute from being shut out for the third time in five games before Pius Suter scored what amounted to be a consolation goal and ended a shutout bid by Dylan Garand, who like the Rangers (8-7-1) was on a four-game losing streak (allowed 15 goals). Garand was on waivers at the start of the season but has been thrust into a top role with Igor Shesterkin (knee) out for the season and backup Joonas Korpisalo (lower-body injury) also out.

* Three in a row is like kryptonite -- The Blues are now 0-3-0 when trying to win three straight.

Last season, the Blues did put together winning streaks or more of three in a row three times from late February on, but as former general manager Doug Armstrong said, those came under "meaningless" games.

In their first five cracks at it last season, they lost every one and were outscored 29-9.

And going back to the previous season, prior to their record-breaking 12-game winning streak they put together from March 15-April 5, 2025, they were 1-7-2 in attempts to win three in a row., and let's face it, without the record winning streak, they likely don't make the playoffs that season.

If they want to be a playoff team again, they better figure out to put winning runs together and put them together often. Right now, this core group simply doesn't get it done.

* Good start to the game -- The Blues seemed to be on the front foot to start the game after the Rangers had to opening 30 seconds to a minute in the Blues zone, St. Louis pushed back and had some really good looks and sustained shifts -- and some lengthy ones -- in the O-zone.

What was missing though, were those areas of crashing the crease, getting bodies in and around the goalie and near the blue paint, burying good looks.

The power play (which we'll get into next) actually looked really good on the first opportunity when Scott Morrow was in the box for the Rangers for high-sticking at 7:44.

* Power play just wasn't good enough -- Look, I understand the Blues had been on a nice run with the man advantage going 8-for-24 and thrusting themselves into the top 10 (ninth) going into the game.

But in going 0-for-6 -- the last one was a meaningless 22 seconds remaining in the game -- in a game which scoring a goal (or more) with the man advantage could have made a huge difference, the Blues laid a big dud.

And the biggest problem was the breakouts and zone entries, which continue to be a problem spot. There's just not enough skill displayed for them to carry the puck in without losing the puck, getting it stripped or not doing so cleanly and it's right back down the ice and they spent the majority of their time simply trying to enter the zone outside of their first opportunity, which generated three of their eight shots.

And when they did have a good look or two the rest of the way, including Pavel Buchnevich's slot chance, retrievals were non-existent, they were one-and-done, and the Rangers, which came in with the third-ranked penalty kill on the season, so you have to give them credit for that, were able to get relatively easy clears.

And to top things off, the Blues were at times their own worst enemy making the ill-advised passes that were coming out of the zone and re-entries were problematic.

The penalty kill, which was good again going 3-for-3 despite the Rangers getting 11 of their 31 shots on goal, did its job, but the flip side of the special teams could have made a difference for the Blues in trying to secure that third straight win.

* Bad line change breaks ice -- The game was scoreless into the second period, so it was turning into one of those contests where mistakes need to be limited and when a scoring chance was available, someone needed to pounce.

The Blues had the offensive zone and puck possession when it suddenly changed hands, and whether Dalibor Dvorsky saw that or not, can't really speak for him, it was his ill-advised line change that enabled the Rangers to drive the the neutral zone with the puck, gain the D-zone of the Blues and find Adam Fox for the ice-breaker at 1:49 of the second period for a 1-0 Rangers lead:

* Turnover leads to clinching goal -- The Blues pulled off the third period comeback against the Detroit Red Wings on Saturday and were in a one-shot game Tuesday, but a turnover led to what amounted to be the game-winner by Oliver Bjorkstrand and a 2-0 New York lead at 9:01 after Mason McTavish lost control of a wobbly puck and turned it over, leading to a mini 3-on-1 that Bjorkstrand buried from the slot:

* Tough night for Fowler-Jiricek pair -- Those two players were on the ice for the first two goals against, and it's only reflective in each being a minus-2.

I get there are a lot of fand right now down on Fowler, but he's had to be the one to nurture a couple young guys as his partner with the shuffling of defensemen and guys being out.

When Colton Parayko comes back, and that could be as early as Thursday, and also Logan Mailloux is ready to play, the Blues need to get him back with Brandon Carlo, someone he began camp with and played really well to start the season with.

Fowler can be more the guy that jumps into the offense, which is suited for his style, and Carlo is the safety net defenseman.

* What is going on with Connor McMichael? -- I can include a number of players in this category, but going into this, and some of the numbers -- or lack thereof -- that were coming from the McMichael stat lines, I really thought this would be the game perhaps the forward would break the schneid and score.

McMichael hasn't scored a goal now since the fourth game of the season (Oct. 8) against the San Jose Sharks and has just two in 15 games; he has eight points, which is OK (not great), but in the past 11 games, McMichael has four assists and just two in the past nine.

He was a minus-2 again Tuesday and just isn't shooting the puck.

In shutout losses to the Carolina Hurricanes on Oct. 24 and Montreal Canadiens on Oct. 27, McMichael had eight shots on goal, which is good. In nine games surrounding those two, he has a grand total of four shots. McMichael is also a minus-8 over his last nine games; his minus-8 on the season is a team worst.

But it's the line in general with McTavish and Jonatan Berggren, which started off the season so productive, has dried up considerably of late. It's gotten stale and perhaps needs a shakeup.

Berggren has just one assist in eight games since scoring Oct. 15 against the Minnesota Wild and McTavish has no points in five games and one goal in the past seven.
