#2815. Phoenix is falling behind, and there’s no room for another slow start

There are plenty of things in life that give people anxiety. For me, it's being late. Or even the threat of being late. Perhaps it's the military man in me, somebody who made the mistake of showing up late to formation during basic training and paid for it in the front-leaning rest position, beads of sweat dripping off my nose and forming a puddle beneath me on the warm pavement at Fort Leonard Wood, Missouri, one June morning. If you're not early, you're late. That's been my mantra ever since.

Unfortunately, that familiar anxiety is beginning to build regarding the Phoenix Suns. This team is 12th in the Western Conference standings after starting 1-4, something we've seen before. At this point last season, the Suns were also 1-4 and were 13th. The difference then is they were only 3.0 games out of first, and due to an odd scheduling start for the Suns and the fact that they have played the fewest games in the NBA, they are 4.5 games back of the top team in the league. That team? The Rockets…

These slow starts create a significant barrier between the Suns and a proverbial train that's already leaving the station, headed toward the postseason. That's right, the Suns are in danger of being late for that train. Anxiety! Because with every passing day and every loss that accumulates, they find themselves falling further and further behind, making it increasingly difficult to hop on board and reach their desired postseason destination.

A season ago, we found ourselves in the same predicament, but the feeling surrounding the team was much different. A big part of that came down to expectations. Nobody really expected the Suns to be a team that could compete at a high level. The prevailing thought was to let the season play out, sell off assets around the trade deadline, and begin building toward the summer of 2026.

Following that 1-4 start, the Suns began putting things together. The sloppy basketball we'd witnessed early in the season became more organized, and the effort that was evident through those first five games finally started translating into wins. I had this to say after they lost 114-113 to the Grizzlies a season ago:

Losing still stings, but this team isn't miles away. The execution needs tightening, and these are the aches that come with learning how to win again.

It's tough to find moral victories in Game 5 when you're sitting at 1–4, but I'll say it anyway. I was entertained. Disappointed, sure. The ending wasn't what anyone wanted. But maybe this year isn't about wins and losses. Maybe it's about how they respond to nights like this.

The feeling this year is different, however. Locally, expectations were higher, even if the national perception remained relatively unchanged. The roster construction was clunky, but you could at least see the vision of what this team wanted to become. Regardless, those expectations change the reality of your situation. While the effort has certainly been present, its consistency has begun to wane, and the execution remains frustratingly inconsistent.

Last season, we could talk ourselves into believing thatDillon Brooks and Jalen Green's absence due to injury was a significant reason for the team's struggles. And perhaps this year, we're doing the same thing with Mark Williams. But the feeling isn't the same. The reality of the situation is slowly sinking in, and it's one in which hope is becoming increasingly difficult to find. This is a team that doesn't control its own draft picks, so finding itself stuck in the middle of the standings is essentially a death sentence.

When you consider the anti-tanking rules that will be in place this year, there's a very real possibility that the Suns could find themselves among the teams with an 8.1% chance of landing the number one overall pick. And how "Suns" would it be for Phoenix to benefit from not being completely horrendous under the league's new draft lottery structure, only to have to hand that pick over to Houston?

Are we there yet? Are we seriously thinking about this already? (I am) When you start 1-4 and look around the standings at the other teams with only one win, seeing the Kings, Nets, Jazz, Bulls, and Bucks alongside you, it's difficult not to get a little frazzled. Because those are the teams that were projected to be bad. It's why we're running to the trade machine trying to find fixes, because you have no room for a down season when there is no benefit to being bad.

I think back to a season ago and the turnaround we witnessed. What the Suns accomplished was impressive, climbing out of 13th place in the Western Conference and playing some incredibly good basketball along the way. For one fleeting night on January 22, 2026, the Suns had fought their way all the way back to the fifth seed in the Western Conference. After starting 1-4, they went 26-13 to reach that point. That is going to be incredibly hard to replicate.

Can this team reproduce any aspect of what we witnessed last year? Can they put together another impressive run that allows them to hop aboard that train headed toward the postseason?

There is hope if you look hard enough. Through their first 5 games a season ago, the Suns had a 120.5 defensive rating (28th) and -9.8 net rating (27th). This season, they are at 124.0 (30th) and -9.8 (25th). After their 1-4 start a season ago, the Suns posted a 112.3 defensive rating (8th) and a +2.1 net rating (13th). It is possible. We've seen it before.

Psst…I'm saying the quiet part out loud…that team can rebound the damn ball…the worst team in rebound percentage last season was the Washington Wizards at 47.1%. The Suns are currently at 45.7%…

We've got a long trip ahead of us, my fellow Suns fans, and we'll spend much of that journey wondering whether this team can recapture any semblance of what it was a season ago. We'll look for solutions in every corner, talking ourselves into the viability of overpaid players like Domantas Sabonis or Dejounte Murray as mediocre saviors. But with so many red flags surrounding the roster construction, it's difficult to shake that familiar feeling of anxiety from being late building in the pit of your stomach.

#2814. What activating Marcus Bryant, releasing Kindle Vildor means for the Patriots

The New England Patriots remain busy modifying the back end of their roster. After releasing safety Elliott Davison on Monday, they made two other depth moves on Tuesday.

Offensive tackle Marcus Bryant was activated from injured reserve to the active roster, with cornerback Kindle Vildor released. The moves mean that the Patriots still have one open spot on their 53-man team — one considered likely to be used on another player set to come off of IR soon, such as wide receiver Mack Hollins.

What else do Tuesday's moves mean, though? Let's take a closer look at them through a big-picture lens.

During the regular season and obviously depending on each individual situation, every NFL team has the opportunity to bring up to eight players back from its injured reserve list. Entering Tuesday, the Patriots had used two such spots already — one on wide receiver A.J. Brown, who returned in Week 6, and the other on Bryant: he had already been declared as a return candidate back when he was first sent to IR in late August.

This, of course, means that Tuesday's activation did not change the eight-player return count; New England is still on 2-of-8 spots used. What the transaction means, however, is that he has rejoined the 53-man roster just in time before his 21-day activation window came to a close.

As a consequence, the Patriots are now five players deep at offensive tackle:

53-man roster (5): Will Campbell (66 | LT), Morgan Moses (76 | RT), Caleb Lomu (74), Dametrious Crownover (68), Marcus Bryant (52)

Practice squad (1): Lorenz Metz (61 | exempt)

Will Campbell and Morgan Moses are entrenched as the starters, with Caleb Lomu having shown intriguing talent whenever entering the field for one of the two. The fourth spot, meanwhile, will be worth keeping an eye on moving forward. If we base the projection off of training camp and preseason, Bryant would be OT4 — in fact, he even had a case for OT3 after the summer — over rookie Dametrious Crownover.

At the very least, Bryant's return gives New England more flexibility and options up front. That includes using Caleb Lomu as an emergency interior lineman, even though the team clearly would prefer to keep him at tackle as much as possible.

Based on Sunday's game against the Miami Dolphins, Vildor's release comes as a minor surprise. While his 13 snaps on the day exclusively came on special teams, he was part of the game day roster as the fifth cornerback over rookies Karon Prunty and Channing Canada.

In fact, the two youngsters were made healthy scratches. And yet, on Tuesday, it was the veteran — who has so far out-snapped both of them on the season — to be let go.

The why has yet to be determined, although it seems that his status might be directly tied to that of cornerback Carlton Davis, who had missed three games due to a neck injury before returning on Sunday. With Davis back in the lineup, roster flexibility seemingly outweighed the need for a seventh player at the position regardless of experience or résumé.

For the time being, New England's cornerback group therefore now looks like this:

53-man roster (6): Christian Gonzalez (0), Carlton Davis III (7), Marcus Jones (25 | PR), Charles Woods (22), Channing Canada (29), Karon Prunty (21)

Practice squad (1): Al'zillion Hamilton (26)

The expectation is that Vildor could be brought back to the practice squad in the near future. If so, he would naturally become a candidate for game-day elevation again should the circumstances require such a move. Already having used one such elevation earlier in the season, the Patriots can bring him up two more times down the stretch this year.

As noted above, his (or Elliott Davison's, depending on how you look at it) roster spot is meanwhile likely to go to a player currently still on injured reserve. Wide receiver Mack Hollins and fullback Reggie Gilliam are eligible to return to practice this week, while offensive lineman Greg Van Roten is among other players who could make a comeback at any point.

#2813. Amanda Serrano and Skye Falzon Face Off In Puerto Rico

Seven-division world champion Amanda Serrano confirmed her final fight will take place on January 9 in Puerto Rico at a news conference Tuesday.

Serrano will face Skye Falzon of Australia in a 12-round fight with three-minute rounds in the main event of a Most Valuable Promotions card airing on DAZN Boxing.

Serrano will attempt to break Christy Martin's all-time knockout record in her final fight.

After 18 years as a professional boxer, seven-division world champion and trailblazer Amanda Serrano has nothing left to prove as she makes a beeline for the Boxing Hall of Fame. But she will shoot for one last goal in her farewell fight on January 9 at the Coliseo de Puerto Rico in San Juan.

Amanda Serrano arrives at the kickout press conference Tuesday in Puerto Rico announcing her retirement fight. Photo: Danny Mosaic, Most Valuable Promotions

The historic event will mark the final professional fight of seven-division world champion Amanda "The MOAT" (Most Of All Time) Serrano (50-4-1, 32 KOs), who will defend her unified WBO and WBA featherweight world titles against Australia's number four WBA contender Skye Falzon (12-2, 7 KOs).

Much like her longtime rival, Katie Taylor, Serrano will enjoy her last fight in front of her devoted fans on her island, a tribute she richly deserves, airing live on DAZN Boxing.

At Tuesday's kickoff event, Serrano and Falzon came face-to-face to discuss the highly anticipated showdown and the significance of Serrano's final fight.

Puerto Rico's first and only undisputed boxing world champion will close her legendary career in front of her home fans, continuing her fight for equality in women's boxing while seeking to break the all-time women's knockout record. Serrano and Falzon were joined by Most Valuable Promotions founders and co-CEOs Nakisa Bidarian and Jake Paul for the official fight announcement in San Juan.

"Back when MVP was just an idea and a promise to put fighters first, Amanda was the first athlete to believe in myself and my partner Jake Paul," recalled Bidarian. "She entrusted us with her career, And she convinced us to go all in on women's boxing.

"Before she even met us, she was a seven-division champion. And since she's met us, she's added more belts. She's become the first and only male or female undisputed champion in the history of Puerto Rico, she's fought for equality and choice for women in the sport of boxing, including giving up her WBC belt to take a stand for three-minute rounds. She's used her platform to push the next generation of male and female Puerto Rican boxers," said Bidarian, noting that her dedication over 18 years has brought her to her final fight, being called "The Last Walk."

"We get to witness the greatness of Amanda Serrano on the last walk to the boxing ring. It's going to be an incredibly loud and an incredibly emotional night, but above all, it will be a celebration of the most decorated boxer in the history of this great land," said Bidarian.

Amanda Serrano poses with opponent Skye Falzon and her Most Valuable Promotions team, Nakisa Bidarian and Jake Paul. Photo: Danny Mosaic, Most Valuable Promotions

Serrano has sold out venues from Madison Square Garden to AT&T Stadium. But she said she always knew her last fight would be in Puerto Rico.

"It means everything, and I always knew that my last fight, my last step will be here in Puerto Rico. You guys been with me from the very beginning, supporting me, loving me, and I've been fighting for you guys," saying her flag is always in her heart. "I just want you guys to celebrate my long journey, and it's gonna be a great night."

Serrano has made no secret of her quest to break Christy Martin's all-time knockout record. Currently, Serrano and Martin are tied with 32 knockouts. Serrano said it's why she made sure her last fight will be 12 three-minute rounds.

"I'm so thankful and grateful to Sky for taking the fight. I mean, she's a warrior too," said Serrano, pushing back on some naysayers who diminish Falzon as an opponent.

"She deserved this opportunity, and I know she's gonna come, and she's gonna try to come upset me here in Puerto Rico," declared Serrano. "But I'm gonna go out there, and I'm gonna give it my all. If the knockout comes, it comes. But I'm gonna enjoy every second of the last time I go into that ring. And you guys are gonna be in for a treat!"

Skye Falzon said she intends to seize the moment against Amanda Serrano. Photo: Danny Mosaic, Most Valuable Promotions

Falzon said she never imagined she'd get a call up to face Serrano.

"I'm a fan of hers. She is the best, and that's just the way it is. To get a call up to fight her was a dream come true," admitted she shed some tears when getting the offer . "The opportunity is something I'm gonna grab hold of, and I just can't wait.

"I've got a great team, and I have a great technical coach. All I got to do is listen and execute what he tells me to do," said Falzon. Falzon said fighting 12 three-minute rounds isn't daunting. She said she regularly trains three-minute rounds in the gym and will now implement them in her sparring.

Serrano said she knew it was time at 38 years old to end her groundbreaking career. But she will remain involved in her management role with MVP and says there is still work to do.

Amanda Serrano vowed to keep fighting for complete equality in women's professional boxing. Photo: Danny Mosaic, Most Valuable Promotions

"The (sanctioning) organizations need to come together and still allow the women to fight the three minutes. I want people to remember me as the game changer, and I gave it my all in the ring. Every time I fall, every time I train, I just left everything in the ring, and I hope you guys see that when I do fight, and I hope you will see that come January 9.

"I'm part of a great team that continues to change the game, and I'm so thankful and blessed.'

When asked what would change if she wins on January 9, Falzon politely pushed back, saying, "Did you say 'if' I win? When I do win on January the ninth, it will be game-changing for me. For everything I've worked for. It's just a dream. If you can't dream it, then you can't achieve it.

"I'm not here just to take part in Amanda's history. You know, I'm here to try to write my own, and I wouldn't be a fighter if I didn't believe I could win," insisted Falzon.

Just like Flora Pili of France who stepped up against Katie Taylor in her final fight, a great champion requires a solid opponent giving her all to create a memorable event. Serrano said that's exactly the way she wants it.

"It's beautiful, and I wouldn't like if any of my opponents will come thinking they're gonna come to lose, I love to fight the best out there. I love that she's gonna come in shape and she's gonna try to take my titles away. These titles are gonna stay here first and foremost. But I love and just for her to go with that mindset knows that when I do beat her, I'm beating the best, Skye Falzon."

Amanda Serrano was denied the all time women's knockout record in her August fight against Lecrecia Manzur of Argentina. Photo: Most Valuable Promotions

It's no secret Serrano is chasing the knockout record. She was denied the knockout in her August fight against Lecrecia Manzur of Argentina. She called it the 'cherry on top' if it comes, but said she will be concentrating on a final victory, and if the knockout comes, it's a bonus.

"I know Puerto Rico is gonna come out. You guys helped me to where I'm at right now, and this is our story. Not just my story, not just my journey. It's our journey, so I would love for you guys to come and celebrate this night with us … I'm gonna give you one last show!"

Asked whether she had a message for young girls and women aspiring to follow in Serrano's footsteps, Serrano said there's no dream too big. "I thought my dreams were big, and I've accomplished my dreams. I accomplished my goals.

"Just believe in yourself. That's all you have to do. Believe in yourself. Train hard. Fight hard in anything you do.

"For me, greatness requires sacrifice. I sacrificed my whole life to be great, and that's why I'm sitting here as one of the most, as some say. Believe in yourself and work hard, and anything and everything is possible," said Serrano.

Tickets go on sale Wednesday, November 4, at 10 a.m. AST (9 a.m. ET) at Ticketera.com for The Last Walk: Serrano vs. Falzon, taking place on Saturday, January 9, 2027, at Coliseo de Puerto Rico in San Juan.

Christensen has played 59 games over the last five seasons. He has seen time at both guard and tackle.

#2811. Fantasy Football Film Room: Buy low on the forgotten Bear; Justin Jefferson is now a boom-or-bust WR2

Fantasy football is inundated with stats and charts (guilty!), but the more context the film provides, the more reliable the data gets. That's why we have a 2.5-hour show called Stats vs. Film every Tuesday at 6 p.m. ET. That show is live — join us in the chat!

This weekly column will look at some of the biggest storylines I picked up by grinding the film.

Previous versions of this column have broken down Colston Loveland's first-read targets and Luther Burden III's exact role in the Bears offense, but Odunze is the forgotten man right now and he feels on the verge of a big game. On Monday Night Football, Odunze had a 4-87-0 line on an 18% target share. That is in line with bye-week WR3/4 value as a baseline, but can it be more?

Odunze is WR3 in 15+ yard receptions (19), only behind Jaxon Smith-Njigba and Tee Higgins. Of the 19 WRs to have at least 11 explosive receptions, Odunze is the lone player to not have a TD on them. What he's done is impossible to do and a very obvious positive regression sign. In this last game, Caleb Williams happened to miss him on a double move go ball heading into halftime that would've been a 50+ yard TD. Odunze has had his fair share of inexcusable drops that should've been scores, but these are the things that should even out moving forward.

The positive plays Monday are what they typically are. He had a 12-personnel dig route on play action where he added a little fake to the outside at the top of his stem for extra separation. These looks are very valuable because there are often 2.5 players in the route, and these throws are often farther down the field. He also had a scramble drill explosive, something that's baked in with Williams being among the best at creating out of structure.

Ben Johnson found a "trick play" that is really easy to execute, but it's enough eye candy for the defense to get lost in the sauce. When D'Andre Swift flips the ball to Caleb, the entire defensive line stops rushing the passer as usual, which gives him extra time and extra space to throw it. The second level zone defenders are also biting and eyeing Swift in the flats more than usual, allowing more space for Odunze to execute his dig route. This is the perfect trick play to me. The risk of ruin is minimized, while still giving the defense something they rarely see.

Quickly on Burden, he played 3 of 21 snaps in heavy personnel. When the Bears established a lead, they went away from 3-WR sets, which is partially explaining the massive splits between halves for Burden this season. He's the WR3 in yards per route in the first half and the WR61 in the second half. When Johnson hasn't isolated him on a first read, the results are worse. That's the reality of a physically dominant yet in-development player. Burden is the WR17 on WR23 usage in games with Caleb this year, so it's no reason to really panic. He's an upside WR2/3 despite the obvious inconsistency.

The backfield is set. Emanuel Wilson was active but didn't play and is on track to be a healthy scratch moving forward, leaving Zach Charbonnet, Jadarian Price and George Holani as the active players. Charbonnet started and played 37% of snaps, leading to 11.6 expected half PPR points (RB22 usage). Price played 48% of snaps, leading to 9.9 expected half PPR points (RB30). Holani played 17%. The trio played on both early and late downs, pass protected, ran routes and carried the ball. It's officially a "let-the-best-man-win" committee, for an organization that held back Kenneth Walker III for years.

So who looked the best? Take a look at this:

Athletically, it's a no contest. Price is so much faster in a straight line and laterally agile. The speed difference is evident on the outside runs, while the lateral ability is easier to see on the cutback runs. That's not the end all, be all, but it absolutely does matter and it's unlikely to change with Charbonnet coming off a February ACL surgery. Price was the one to receive the pitch on a sweep, so the coaching staff obviously agrees.

That said, Price did have two reps where he probably could've done a lot more with the look. One of them was by not giving his center the time to climb to the next level. He instead jammed into a crowded lane, where a more patient and crafty runner would've cut back for a nice gain. The other rep was attempting to bounce it outside of nickel CB Kyler Gordon, when he could've hit his inside shoulder hard for a potential nice gain. Neither were egregious, but the top-end veterans treat them differently.

Charbonnet's runs were more textbook, but there was space to carve out real explosives if he was simply faster. Both of the runs in the video above showcase that glaring flaw. I'd understand it if they'll always value Charbonnet's size and quality decision making in short yardage, but the Seahawks would do well if they continued giving Price a lot of the early down and between the 20s runs to increase their odds of an explosive and to give Price more time on task to develop.

Beyond that, the Seahawks have a very obvious WR depth problem. Jaxon Smith-Njigba is so good that he'll get home regardless, but teams are either straight-up bracketing him or shading a safety over the top of him on most reps. Sam Darnold isn't seeing the field well after the first read, and the depth pass-catchers aren't talented enough to make the reads clearer. It's part of the reason why JSN's role is slightly more underneath this year and very much why Darnold isn't playing nearly as well in 2026. I don't think this is OC Brian Fleury's issue per se, but they need to find another WR at the deadline or find a breakout at RB to counteract this glaring pass-game issue.

The Rams offense has looked a lot different this past month. A lot of that is having zero depth at WR to overcome the Davante Adams injury, but I'm not convinced that's the entire story here.

From Weeks 5-8, Stafford is 1 of 9 on throws with 20+ air yards. The 11% completion percentage and raw completions are obviously alarming but so is the attempt total! He is QB27 in deep attempts on QB6 total attempts during this four-game span.

On tape, Stafford doesn't look fully healthy right now. Some of his sideline and downfield attempts lack his usual zip, and his throwing motion is feeling a little more Philip Rivers-like. He had a sideline completion to TuTu Atwell, but even that one really could've been a score had he led him more. That was more of a line drive that went to his inside shoulder that caused him to hit the brakes a bit. Some of his throws on the move are drifting low and away. And then other throws that are to the intermediate aren't coming with his normal spot-on accuracy.

I wasn't surprised to see Monday's report that his elbow is sore. That's what his throwing motion looks like right now, and the offense being extremely quick-hitting right now is further evidence of just that.

He's fantasy QB16 right now, and I'm not sure if the Rams 3-TE set offense with Puka Nacua working on a 3.4 average depth of target sans Adams is one that's going to lead to massive fantasy days, even if the offense is still moving the ball well. The margins are just a lot tighter in this current offense, and the defense is good enough to keep games on the lower end.

On the Nacua front, he hasn't had a reception of 20+ air yards since Week 4 and has had one target of 15+ air yards in the two most recent games. He's probably not comfortable completely opening up his stride on downfield routes right now, paired with whatever is up with Stafford's elbow. That's OK. He's still the WR3 overall on WR1 usage since returning in Week 4 and is up to 41% targets in the two games without Adams. That'll do.

I still can't believe Lamar Jackson is headed to IR because of a damn thumb injury on the first snap of a showdown with Josh Allen. For Tyler Huntley (turf toe) to also go down is all the more sickening. The Ravens play on Thursday night, so it's too quick to make a move for a Jacoby Brissett or other veteran QB. They'll see what they have with Fagnano again, and it'll be an uphill climb with the Jaguars defense on deck.

Fagnano is a big (6-foot-3, 230 pounds) statue quarterback from UConn, where he finished last year with a 28:1 TD:INT ratio. He went undrafted and has been the QB3 despite not matching up with the Ravens' athletic QB room. He is definitely more of a pocket passer who will struggle to throw on the move or scramble around. That is doubly true now that he's practicing with an ankle brace on. The Declan Doyle offense doesn't need a Lamar Jackson-level athlete to execute, but the margins are tighter, especially behind an offensive line that is in pretty rough shape.

Quickly on that, new center Cam Jurgens has to do a better job of picking up stunts and games. There were too many wasted reps on that alone, and the rest of the right side of the line also had their own issues on one-on-one opportunities. The Ravens really need first-round left guard Vega Ioane back, and he has a chance to return this week.

Fagnano did have a couple of really nice completions to the sideline. They came with anticipation and quality arm talent. That's encouraging in what should be a heavy dropback environment in Week 9 with Jacksonville's defense facing the highest neutral pass rate allowed. Fagnano does look like he can stretch the field horizontally at the very least.

Zay Flowers does a lot of his damage over the middle. It was not Flowers' best work in Week 8. He had a drop on a shallow with room to run. He also fell down on a 3rd-and-8 first-read look before a target was thrown to him. The Bills also bracketed him on another key third down, leading to an incompletion. Fagnano did attempt a big sideline throw to him, but an edge rusher knocked the ball upon release. His lone interception was to Flowers, where he simply stared him down, allowing a zone linebacker to slide his way. My takeaway coming from this game is there is more of a chance for fantasy points for Flowers than what his goose egg would suggest. Fagnano appears to understand the assignment of throwing it to Flowers when he can, but the Jaguars defense will have Travis Hunter and a well-coached defense to squat on these middle-of-the-field throws.

I'd view Flowers as a fantasy starter but more of the WR3 variety than a must start. Here are his splits without Jackson since last year. Please note that last week did fall into the "with" Jackson game. The true split is closer to 13.5 versus 10.5.

Derrick Henry's splits are also problematic. He's the RB28 on RB17 usage this month without Jackson, and his efficiency is night and day depending on if the star QB is out there to threaten defenses himself. I don't trust the OL talent when they simply line up under center, and Henry is far more likely to be phased out due to game script now. He takes the bigger positional tumble in rankings without Jackson and with the Jaguars' dominant run defense. The Ravens have a pathetic team total:

Everyone. The scale I'm using is: Is Justin Jefferson happy? There's no reason he shouldn't be, as very clearly one of the best players in the NFL. Well, right now he's mad and not speaking to the media. Meanwhile, Kevin O'Connell is talking and making pretty direct comments about Kyler Murray: "I thought he made some big-time throws. There were some positives, but there are still some plays we're leaving out there that will help with the overall rhythm of the game. We've gotta continue to find the best way to get him comfortable."

Agree!!! There are some turned down throws beyond his first read, like this one to Jefferson:

Then there are inaccuracies. Murray had a touchdown opportunity up the seam on a correct backside read, but he sailed it. The play below was out of structure, but it's the same result. Sailed. In previous weeks, his intermediate timing-paced routes to Jefferson were missed. Murray has never been considered perfect on these intermediate throws, but this is a step down from his usual for whatever reason.

O'Connell is also on my radar. Even during the Sam Darnold high-flying era, O'Connell left his QB out to dry by asking him to hold onto the ball for downfield concepts without a checkdown available. This is a very difficult offense to run, and it needs an aggressive thrower with stars at receiver to execute. This OL is solid, but it's not elite, making the holding onto the ball part harder.

KOC also doesn't do enough to protect Jefferson when teams are doubling him. The Lions did a great job with Jefferson in Week 8 by playing outside leverage with a CB at the line and sinking a safety to the inside. Jefferson isn't in stacks, in motion, or on the backside of these concepts enough to overcome what is a pretty obvious way to handle this Vikings offense. Murray can't simply throw it up to him in these looks without risk of an interception. The Jordan Addison routes are too often derailed, too. If he played like a first-round WR, then Jefferson wouldn't see these looks nearly as often. This is how Tee Higgins is producing like a top-five receiver in a similar situation.

Jefferson has, by far, the least blame, but he did drop a pass and could've made a highlight touchdown grab up the seam in this past game. He has a 78-855-5 receiving line over his last 17 games for a 50 YPG average. He was at 96 YPG before that. His effort dipped at times throughout this span, but it hasn't been an issue this year. I totally understand if his frustrations start to spill over if this doesn't get corrected. In the meantime, Jefferson has lost his fantasy WR1 label. He's a boom-bust WR2 while the Vikings dip to 31st in passing yards per game on the No. 25 neutral pass rate.

#2809. Why Did Spire Snub Corey Day as Carson Hocevar’s NASCAR Replacement?

Carson Hocevar, who was suspended by Spire Motorsports. Getty

Spire Motorsports named Justin Haley to drive the No. 77 Chevrolet at Homestead-Miami Speedway on Sunday, replacing suspended Carson Hocevar. But why Haley? Why did Spire pass over rising young star Corey Day?

With Day chasing an O'Reilly Auto Parts Series title the day before, the pick of a 180-start veteran raises an obvious question about Spire's 2027 plans.

Spire suspended Hocevar for the Homestead finale over his actions at Martinsville Speedway on Nov. 1. SMT data showed Hocevar brake-checking Austin Cindric's No. 2 Team Penske Ford several times after a restart, damaging the nose and ending Cindric's day. Cindric finished 36th. NASCAR later fined Hocevar $100,000 and docked him 25 driver points, but Spire acted on its own.

"Racing at the Cup level is a privilege, and it comes with an expected level of professionalism from everyone who represents Spire Motorsports," co-owner Jeff Dickerson said, according to Spire Motorsports' own statement. Hocevar called his suspension "an embarrassing day" and said he "crossed the line." He expects to return in 2027.

GettyCarson Hocevar, who was suspended by Spire Motorsports.

Spire has not publicly addressed the decision not to race Day in Hocevar's place.

Haley delivered the organization's first Cup win in the rain-shortened 2019 Daytona race, driving the No. 77 in only his third career start. He ran full time for Spire in 2025 in the No. 7 and finished 31st in points. The team did not bring him back for 2026. Haley now runs full time for Kaulig Racing in the Truck Series, which leaves him free for a one-off Cup start.

Haley had 180 Cup starts, one win, 18 top-10 finishes and no poles through 2025. His best points finish was 22nd in 2022 with Kaulig. Day is set to make his Cup debut in 2027, driving part time for Spire.

Born April 28, 1999, in Winamac, Indiana, Haley started in quarter midgets at age 9. He took the 2016 K&N Pro Series East championship, then won three Truck Series races in 2018 and reached the Championship 4. He added four wins in what was then the Xfinity Series, three in 2020 and one in 2021.

NASCAR Slams Carson Hocevar With Harsh Punishment For Martinsville Race Actions

Day, 20, was born Nov. 28, 2005, in Clovis, California, the son of West Coast sprint car racer Ronnie Day. He climbed through dirt bikes, micro sprints and sprint cars, and won the 2023 King of the West championship. In 2024, he became the youngest winner of the Turkey Night Grand Prix.

Day is a Hendrick Motorsports development driver under a long-term contract. Hendrick has reached the four-car charter limit, so Day is slated for five or six Cup starts for Spire in 2027 in open entries. Those races are planned for the middle to late part of the season. Day has not yet run a Cup race.

His first full O'Reilly season in the No. 17 HendrickCars.com Chevrolet has produced four wins, at Talladega, Dover, Las Vegas and Charlotte. After Martinsville, Day sits third with 2,413 points, 23 behind leader Justin Allgaier and one behind Jesse Love. Carson Kvapil trails Allgaier by 31 points and Sheldon Creed by 42. Five drivers remain eligible for the title, and Day has described it as essentially a must-win situation.

The O'Reilly championship race goes green Saturday at 5 p.m. EST. The Cup finale follows Sunday. Spire has not said whether that schedule played into its decision, but Haley has no O'Reilly Series commitment that weekend, and Day's Cup work with the team does not begin until 2027.

#2808. Jerry Jones claims (incorrectly) that there's "no correlation" between coaching pay and winning

Jerry Jones has said that the size of the check he would write to guarantee another Super Bowl win would be “embarrassing.”

Jones, when it comes to the resources he is willing to invest in the team, is cheap. He won’t fire coaches who have buyouts. And he won’t pay top dollar for new coaches.

The owner and G.M. of the Cowboys defended his approach during his latest visit to 105.3 The Fan in Dallas by making the nonsensical claim that there’s no connection between coaching pay and winning.

“In the NFL, this is after years of looking at all the teams and all the coaches, there is no — there is zero correlation between how much you pay a coach, or how many coaches you have, as to winning,” Jones said. “There is no correlation. And that’s a random sample of years and all 32 teams involved.  So put that in your hopper and smoke it.”

He’s just wrong. Hiring a proven coach, and paying him accordingly, can change everything. We’ve seen it, time and again.

Andy Reid instantly turned the Chiefs into consistent contenders, and three-time Super Bowl winners. Sean Payton has made the Broncos into an annual contender, positioning him to become the first coach to win a Super Bowl for two different teams. Most recently, John Harbaugh has immediately changed everything for the Giants.

Harbaugh’s success will result in his former rival, Mike Tomlin, fending off teams willing to pay him upwards of $30 million per year to swap out his earpiece and microphone on the NBC sideline set for the Sony headset along the white stripe.

Jones prefers to speculate, when it comes to coaches. In lieu of paying for a coach who has proven that he can win, Jones wants to roll the dice on an unproven commodity in the hopes of maximizing the return on his investment.

“You can be pretty creative in hiring your coaches,” Jones said. “You can be creative sometime when you don’t even know you’re hiring the coaches.  And so I’ve had some good experience of hiring people who have never been in the NFL. I’ve had some experience of hiring young coaches, older coaches, and so, of course, you constantly remind me, as you should, of what my record is. And I understand that.  But I also know that the next time you throw the pick, if you were the old California gold miner, you may hit the Comstock Lode. And then it is gloryhole.”

“Creative” is another way of saying “cheap.” Buy low, and reap the benefit when the less expensive option yields results. And stick with him until his contract expires, to avoid having to pay two coaches at the same time.

It would be different if there truly was no correlation between quality (and cost) of coach and winning. But there ABSOLUTELY is a connection.

Still, Jerry prefers to dig through the bargain bin in search of an abandoned Botticelli. His own twist on “if you have to ask you can’t afford it” is “if you have to ask I don’t want it.”

It’s no surprise. In the early days of the salary cap, members of the media often pointed out that there’s no cap on coaching pay. In time, the NFL realized, “Oh shit, there’s no cap on coaching pay.”

That has led to what some believe is an effort to collectively keep coaching pay from growing at the rate that it should. And some in league circles are convinced that Jerry Jones is the ringleader of what is, as a practical matter, an effort to collude.

Even if he’s not going to pay top-of-market money for a coach, the higher the market goes, the more expensive it gets across the board. The rising tide, in other words, raises all dinghies. And Jerry would much rather spend his money on his aircraft carrier that has been retrofitted into a luxury hotel.

That’s his prerogative. But Cowboys fans — who fork over their money and who devote their time and attention to watching games on TV and consuming Cowboys content — should understand what’s going on. Jerry Jones is pinching pennies when it comes to paying coaches. As a result, he’s often getting what he isn’t paying for.

His mindset is highly relevant to the looming wreckage of the 2026 season. If coach Brian Schottenheimer has guaranteed money left on his contract for 2027, he will not be fired. And if Jones can avoid owing Schottenheimer a buyout, Jones will resist joining the pursuit of Tomlin, who admitted on the most recent edition of Football Night in America that he grew up a fan of the Cowboys.

Actually, that could make Jerry think that deep-seated rooting interest will give him a chance at hiring Tomlin on the cheap. Because cheap does as cheap is. And, when it comes to his team (and specifically to his coaches), Jerry Jones is cheap.

#2807. Jets sign OL Blake Freeland off Bengals' practice squad

The Jets signed offensive tackle Blake Freeland off the Bengals' practice squad, the team announced Tuesday.

#2806. ‘This Isn’t on Jerry Jones,’ ‘You Guys Are Blowing It’ — Cris Collinsworth Comes to Cowboys Owner’s Defense Amid Noise Around $17 Billion Franchise

Jerry Jones has heard plenty about what is wrong with the Dallas Cowboys, and another Sunday loss has given fans even more reasons to question his decisions.

The frustration is understandable after Dallas let a game slip away that it seemed to have under control. But while much of the criticism has landed on the owner, Cris Collinsworth believes the players and coaches need to take a long, hard look at themselves first.

Collinsworth argued that Jones has already provided the personnel changes the Cowboys requested.

In Week 8, Dallas fell to 3-5 after surrendering a 14-point fourth-quarter lead in a 33-30 overtime loss to the Arizona Cardinals. Their defense is struggling, as evidenced by the fact that they rank dead last in PFN's NFL Defense Impact Metric%20assigns,red%20zone%20and%20third%20downs.).

The loss ended a remarkable streak: Dallas had previously won all 191 home games since 1991 in which it held a lead of at least 14 points during the fourth quarter, including the playoffs.

"This isn't on Jerry Jones. We did our job. You guys are blowing it," Collinsworth said. He then pointed to the additions made by the front office, including a new defensive coordinator and cornerback Joey Porter Jr., whom Dallas acquired from Pittsburgh.

"If I were Jerry, I'd probably do exactly what he's done, which is, no, I paid all you guys. You said you needed this. You needed a new defensive coordinator. You needed Joey Porter. You needed all these different things. I got them. You guys go fix it," he added.

Not me. I'm not going out and doing something again. You guys are crazy. You guys get yourself in a closed room and figure it out," he said.

#2805. What We Learned from the Spurs win over the Jazz

Somewhere in the second quarter, with the Spurs up one and about to be up more, I realized I wasn't watching the game. I was, instead, watching myself not watch the game, which is a worse hobby and the one I'm apparently best at. The game was fine. The game was exactly what it said on the label: a nondescript tilt between two teams already headed down different tracks. The shine of October basketball had worn off completely and, like summer turning to fall, the Grind had arrived.

As a first quarter blowout turned into a 2nd quarter snoozer, my brain began to wander a bit. I could be anywhere in the world right now. A plastic stool in Hanoi, a bowl of pho, a little light rain. A mountain pass in the Andes, thin air, nobody asking me for a take. Man, I should be reading books. Seeing films. I should be the kind of person whose passport has all those cool stamps in it. This one precious life the universe handed me was unraveling in real time as Ace Bailey stepped into a 25-foot pull-up three to cut the Jazz's deficit to one.

Then Stephon Castle missed a layup and I almost decided to call it a night. It was as annoying as it was unremarkable and I just didn't need it anymore. I reached for the remote and, as I did, Victor Wembanyama, who a minute earlier had similarly flubbed an alley-oop, grabbed the rebound and tipped it back in. Twenty seconds after Bailey's three. No celebration. Barely a facial expression.

I'd love to tell you that's the moment I'd been looking for, but that would be too tidy. What I'd been searching for was a hook. A story, a cause, a catalyst. Anything to hold off the particular ennui of being a fan of a good team. And Wemby wasn't even good last night, not by any stretch. Five of fifteen. Zero for five from three. Five turnovers. He was in that mode he gets in from time to time where he decides the Key to Victory is to simply chuck it.

The Spurs won by 22 anyway, not because of a miracle but because this roster has more than one way to be competent. The Spurs are, mercifully, more than the sum of their parts.

Not that the parts are bad. Quite the contrary. Dylan Harper scored all 17 of his points before the third quarter was half over. Stephon Castle is an animal. De'Aaron Fox looks like the guy we paid for. Tarris Reed continues to grab rebounds in a way that feels like it's going to be important (seven in about seven minutes last night). Every wing combination we've thrown on the court is its own kind of fun.

I mean, look at Harrison Barnes. A man as old as time itself (he's four years younger than me) had an insanely active night. He knocked down threes from 29, 25 and 24 feet, he ran around, he caused trouble. Early in the fourth he got tangled up with Brice Sensabaugh, slid across the floor, got back up, and just stared at him while the refs reviewed it in slow motion. I'm told that clip racked up 140,000 views on the world wide web within about an hour.

The thing about the Grind is that it doesn't have to mean anything, which means that everything within the Grind gets to be exactly what it is. A tip dunk. A well executed outlet pass. An old man staring down Brice Sensabaugh. Like, I didn't come out of the womb craving the Larry O'Brien trophy. I just was at my breakfast table in 1996 watching Michael Jordan dunk on Rik Smits during a SportsCenter rerun, and some part of me said, with zero strategic vision, "Yes. More of that." More of that was the entire ask and, frankly, it still is.

Look at us. You and me. Like I said, we could be anywhere in the world right now, but we're right here with the Spurs and the Jazz on a Monday night in November. The stools in Hanoi will still be there someday, but a precocious superstar, having the worst night of his season, is putting a ball back through the hoop like a child tucking in his stuffed animals for the night.

You don't have to impress some greater importance on a moment like that to make it mean something. You were there. It's a part of you now and you got to live it.

If that isn't special, I don't know what is.

Dylan Harper seems almost casually good at basketball. It's remarkable and breathtaking, these things he's doing, but it also has that effect of seeming effortless when he's casually dismantling another team. In the second quarter he stole the ball and in a blink Vassell was dunking off his pass. In the third, he stole it again and Stephon was finishing an alley-oop before I'd even registered it. He added 8 assists and 7 rebounds on top of all his scoring. I find myself almost taking it for granted at this point, and we've really only started to scratch the surface of what he's capable of. Scary to think about! (For our opponents. For us it's just exceedingly cool and fun.)

I know it's just the Jazz, grain of salt and all that, but I never get tired of seeing the Spurs get out on the break and run. They won fast-break points 26-9, against a Jazz team that turned it over just 11 times, a season low for Utah, so a lot of that was pure rebound-and-go. They also won the paint 52-36 and opened the game on an 18-2 run. Run for days my boys. Grab the ball and never stop running. Only good can come from this. Open looks from three, easy paths to the rim, potential foul trouble for the opponents. Push that pace. Push that pace. PUSH THAT PACE.

This is not how development works and, to be clear, I don't know what I'm talking about, but I absolutely crave Tarris Reed minutes. I want him to become a Sasquatch, Godzilla, King Kong, Loch Ness, Goblin, ghoul and a zombie with no conscience. I want basketballs to fear him checking into the game because they're about to get rebounded to within an inch of their lives.

I love a wire-to-wire win. Next time you hear me complaing about a wire to wire win feel free to come to my house and take an ice cold waterloo out of my fridge as payment for my sins.

I'm a man of all seasons, to be clear, but right now, yes, I am currently in a Waterloo phase of my Sparkling Water career.

There is a particular Black Cherry flavor I've grown fond of and I just can't stay away at this point. It scratches this weird nostalgic part of my brain that yearns for a Blue Sky soda I had in my youth. Do you remember Blue Sky soda?

Yeah, the cans were cool. I don't know. I think I had just never experienced the concept of a soda that existed outside the mainstream world of the Coca-Cola and Pepsi family trees, so when I went on vacation and saw this crazy looking can with a cherry sitting on a cloud it just… broke my brain a little bit and I had to have it.

Who could say? I was six years old. I'd left the summer heat in Texas and woken up the next day in a Colorado mountain town sipping an alien soda from heaven. Maybe it was good, maybe it wasn't, but it stirred something deep in my soul that I think I've been searching for ever since.

And Black Cherry Waterloos scratch that itch for you?

They do. Publix also pretty regularly throws them in Buy One Get Ones for me and who am I to turn down a deal like that.

#2803. St. Joseph shuts out Ridgefield, claims second straight FCIAC girls soccer championship

FAIRFIELD - The FCIAC title on the line alone Tuesday night would have served as motivation enough for girls soccer teams St. Joseph and Ridgefield.

Both teams were looking to add another championship banner, having combined for 16 league titles. Neither team had lost a game coming in. And both teams play outstanding defense.

Both defenses were again on display at Tetreau-Davis Field. In the end, it was St. Joseph with the 2-0 shutout. Sophia Santorsola was the difference, scoring late in each half for the Cadets.

"I think we are the best defense in the state," St, Joseph goalkeeper Cailyn O'Hara said. "There's no argument about that. And we proved it."

St. Joseph has been No. 1 in the GametimeCT power rankings since defeating Greenwich in October. Ridgefield is currently third. St. Joseph is also No. 1 in the Class LL/L state coaches poll.

The teams played to a scoreless tie on Sept. 29.

It was the second-straight FCIAC tournament championship for the Cadets (16-0-2) and fourth in five years. St. Joseph has now appeared in the last five finals and has eight league titles overall.

So it remains the same: you still have to go through the Cadets to claim the FCIAC crown.

"People can hate us, people can hate me, but the bottom line is this: five straight years to the finals, four out of five years, we won it, and that's because of those players," St. Joseph coach Jack Nogueira said.

This was the 16th shutout for St. Joseph. O'Hara, who will play soccer at Sacred Heart University next season, allowed just two goals, one each to Trumbull and Greenwich, the latter on a penalty kick.

"This is a microcosm of what we've been all year," Nogueira said. "The ability to score goals when we need to and defend."

Ridgefield (15-1-3), which has 15 shutouts, allowed eight goals, including four to Greenwich in two games.

"I think we can use this experience, our first final in a few years, to hopefully learn from it, use it as motivation to go on a good state run," Ridgefield coach Barry O'Reilly said.

St. Joseph and Ridgefield are the top two seeds in the CIAC Class LL bracket.

Ridgefield tested O'Hara and the St. Joseph defense, but were not able to get the equalizer in the second half.

Meanwhile, Santorsola was able to notch her pair of goals. The first one came with 63 seconds left in the first half on an assist from Sara Wicker. Ridgefield goalkeeper Cathryn Mills got a hand on Wicker's shot, but she could not control it.

Santorsola then dumped it into the open net.

"I passed it to her (Wicker) first and she took her shot," Santorsola said. "I saw it come out, I knew I had to be there for the rebound. Second goal, I knew there was not much time left, but I saw the opportunity and I just took it."

Santorsola's second goal came with 1:09 left in the game.

"The FCIAC is definitely so full of a lot of talent," O'Hara said. "Props to Ridgefield, I think they're an amazing team and they gave us a bit of a run for our money. But to be able to go back to back is something to be honored and looked at."

The sophomore scored both goals for the Cadets, both late in each half.

"Sophia does what Sophia does for us, she pressures the ball, she's all over the field and has an innate ability to find garbage goals. That's no disrespect to her because a lot of people made a lot of money scoring garbage goals." - St. Joseph girls soccer coach Jack Nogueira

#2801. Sione Vaki emerges as Lions' change-of-pace back to pair with Jahmyr Gibbs

Even though the Lions have only played seven games and most teams have played eight, Lions running back Jahmyr Gibbs is leading the NFL with 191 touches — a huge workload that raises questions about whether Detroit needs a player to share carries with Gibbs, the way David Montgomery had done before he was traded to Houston.

Vaki had a career-high 11 carries and a career-high 65 yards in Sunday’s win over the Vikings, and his direct running style was a valuable change of pace for the Lions to have while Gibbs got some breathers between his own 23 carries for 153 yards.

Lions coach Dan Campbell emphasized after the game that the Lions told Vaki they needed him to step up against the Vikings, and Vaki answered the bell.

“Vaki was one of the guys we challenged this week going into this game,” Campbell said. “We were going to need him. He needed to show up. He was going to need to get some carries for us, some significant carries. They needed to be real yards, and he was going to need to be able to pass protect for us. And I thought he took a big step today. That’s a huge step. He gained a lot of trust today, a ton.”

Lions quarterback Jared Goff said the entire offense is excited about what Vaki can bring.

“Vaki ran his tail off,” Goff said. “And when he’s able to do that, it just gives Gibbs that much more of a breath on the sideline to come back and do his thing at his full capacity. And the O-line loved it. They loved the challenge of, like, we’re going to keep leaning on you guys. And I thought they played great.”

The Lions think they have the best running back in the league in Gibbs. If Vaki can prove to be one of the better backup running backs in the league, the Lions’ running game will be in very good shape.

A treble winner with FC Bayern, Corentin Tolisso scored and assisted in Lyon's 2-0 win against Angers to stay in second place.

In contrast, Marseille had a tough start to the Ligue 1 season before climbing to sixth with their fourth win on the trot. Amine Harit scored his third league goal of the season days before his return to Germany as Marseille face Bayer Leverkusen in their next test on Thursday.

Nicolo Tresoldi scored and assisted for Club Brugge in their 4-2 win at Waregem. The German-Italian striker is now the sole leader of the scoring chart in Belgium's top-flight league with eight goals. Although it's not yet clear whether he made a decision on his international allegiance, Tresoldi could be named in Jürgen Klopp's upcoming squad this week.

Former Bundesliga striker Wout Weghorst scored four goals for Twente in their 9-0 win over ten-man Excelsior in Eredivisie. Leaders Feyenoord dropped points as Ajax and the defending champions PSV narrowed the gap at the top.

Liga Portugal leaders Porto extend their perfect record with former Bundesliga striker Andre Silva scoring fourth goal of the campaign, while Samuel Essende scored twice for Young Boys despite his team's 5-3 home defeat to Zurich in Switzerland.

The DR Congo international, who has joined the club from Augsburg at the start of the year, leads the scoring chart in Swiss Super League with 14 goals. Meanwhile, fellow former Bundesliga strikers and Nigerian duo Gift Orban and Victor Osimhen are top of Super Lig ranks.

Osimhen's Galatasaray will face Stuttgart in the Champions League on Tuesday, while fellow Turkish side Trabzonspor are Freiburg's next opponents in the Europa League. #2798. Rams Get Matthew Stafford Update Amid ‘Interesting’ Myles Garrett Situation

Matthew Stafford was spotted with ice on his right elbow following the Los Angeles Rams' Week 8 win over the Los Angeles Chargers.

However, NFL Network's Ian Rapoport does not believe there is any reason for concern.

"I haven't heard anything to have me believe that Stafford won't be ready," Rapoport said on "The Pat McAfee Show" on November 3. "I would expect him to be good to go–unless there's other information that I don't know, which does happen."

Despite the bottom-line positivity, Rapoport's update on Stafford adds to the nebulous nature of the situation.

Rams head coach Sean McVay did not provide much clarity following the reports.

"He's had some soreness there," McVay told reporters on November 2. "I think he'd be better equipped, once you guys talk to him, to answer that. I can't attest to stuff that I'm not necessarily aware of."

Stafford is still tracking to speak with the media for his typical availability on Wednesday, at least based on McVay's comments, which is an encouraging sign in its own right.

Of course, Stafford is hardly the Rams' only injury concern.

In addition to Stafford, Rapoport was also asked about the Rams getting Myles Garrett back into the mix. Garrett has been sideined since Week 1, undergoing arthroscopic knee surgery and landing on injured reserve.

Rapoport said Garrett's situation is "an interesting one."

"I thought he was coming back last week. Just didn't get to a point where he could feel comfortable enough on the knee. So, it was like, 'All right, don't rush it. Stretch run. Be ready this week," Rapoport said. "I was told, when he gets on the field for practice, that it should all happen very quick. So, I think this week is in play, but we need to see how he actually responds. But I think it's, finally after like two months, in a pretty good place."

McVay spoke candidly about the Rams taking a deliberate approach with Garrett after announcing they had opened his 21-day practice window but refraining from much else.

"I want to be careful to even make set any sort of expectations. I think just taking it a day at a time is going to be the best way that we can approach it; I think that's the right thing for him, and really just to see how he responds" McVay said. "But I think it's a real positive that we're starting his clock."

The Rams have yet to see Garrett record a counting stat since trading for him this offseason.

One issue for Stafford, Garrett, and the Rams is that they are not getting the full benefits of typical NFL scheduling, even beyond their Week 1 trip to Australia to face the 49ers. Stafford's wife, Kelly Stafford, lamented the situation.

"I had to take a second off my workout because I keep getting updates and words like, 'Oh, well, after the bye week, they'll be back. After the bye week, he'll be healthy. Not for the Rams though, because they didn't get a bye week. And if you're new to the NFL, a bye week is when they have the week off. So, if they play Sunday, then they don't play the next Sunday, then they play the Sunday after that," Stafford said in a video on her Instagram story on November 3.

"It's a lot of time. It's good, it's good for teams, right? We can get people back who are injured. I'm confused on how we forgot to give us a bye week. If you guys want to go look at the schedule and show me where there is a bye week–where there are 14 days in between games–I'd be ecstatic. But doesn't exist. Thanks, NFL!"

Kelly Stafford shared two other posts to her IG story.

One of the other posts said the Rams' bye was still loading and another noting Stafford is in his 18th NFL season.

The Rams have a bye. It is in Week 11. However, they will have just 10 days off from their Week 10 matchup against the Arizona Cardinals–who defeated LA in Week 6–and their Week 12 showdown with the Green Bay Packers.

#2796. 49ers trade targets: 4 players San Francisco could pursue at NFL trade deadline

The San Francisco 49ers have an opportunity to improve their roster ahead of the league's trade deadline on Nov. 10.

The Niners return from the bye week ready to bring their former defensive lineman DeForest Buckner back into the fold after completing a trade with the Indianapolis Colts, bringing the 32-year-old back to the Bay Area.

The deal for Buckner was just a start; San Francisco isn't expected to be done yet. The 49ers' main concern is depth and making sure they have enough bodies to make it through the regular season and into the postseason.

The 49ers are 6-1 but have dealt with a ton of injuries throughout the year, mainly at defensive line and wide receiver. Those are areas where San Francisco could use more depth.

TRADE GRADES: Who won the DeForest Buckner trade?

The Niners tried to bring back former receiver Jauan Jennings in a trade with the Minnesota Vikings, but Jennings denied the trade.

Receivers that have been out for the 49ers include Ricky Pearsall, De'Zhaun Stribling, DeMarcus Robinson and tight end Jake Tonges. Deebo Samuel Sr. is expected to be ready to go after sitting with a right hamstring injury. KhaDarel Hodge was signed to the 49ers' practice squad and the 32-year-old wide receiver might have a chance to play against the Las Vegas Raiders on Nov. 9.

49ers receiver Brandon Aiyuk hired a new agent, bringing Drew Rosenhaus on his team in an attempt to return to the NFL. Still, San Francisco is likely not an option.

Although things aren't terrible on the defensive side of the ball, San Francisco could use upgrades defensively, at least more help in their pass rush and on the edge.

The 49ers defense is 20th in sacks and 27th in takeaways, including 29th in interceptions and 19th in forced fumbles. San Francisco's run defense allows 89 yards per game, the 5th-fewest in the league. They're third in total rush yards with 623 through 7 games. They've allowed the fewest rushing touchdowns, three.

Still, the injuries the 49ers have experienced still affect them. They've been dealt injuries to Nick Bosa, Alfred Collins, C.J. West, Sam Okuayinonu, Mikail Kamara and Andrew Farmer II. All have missed time going in and out of the lineup. The 49ers only recently got Mykel Williams back from an ACL injury.

Still, the 49ers may consider a move ahead of the Nov. 10 deadline. Who could they target if they keep buying? Here's a look at the top trade options San Francisco could pursue.

The 49ers need a WR and Jaguars wide receiver Brian Thomas Jr. could use a fresh start after a slow start to the season. In his third season, Thomas, through seven games, has recorded 10 receptions, 117 yards and two touchdowns. He averages 11.7 yards per catch.

He earned a Pro Bowl selection during his rookie year in 2024 for season stats of 87 catches, 1,282 yards and 10 touchdowns in 17 games. Then he had a sophomore slump. He played 14 games and only tallied 48 catches, 707 yards and two touchdowns.

San Francisco needs more young weapons in their aging WR room after all the injuries. Thomas would be a dream to team up with Mike Evans, Deebo Samuel and tight end George Kittle.

Khalil Mack is 35 and was the 5th overall pick selected by the then-Oakland Raiders in the 2014 NFL Draft. The former NFL Defensive Player of the Year is older but can still provide a spark as a vet. Mack built his career as a pass rusher, compiling 116 career sacks and ranking seventh all-time among active players.

Mack has done just about everything in the NFL, except win a Super Bowl. He was the 2016 Defensive Player of the Year, a nine-time Pro Bowl selection and a three-time All-Pro linebacker. He might thrive in a role where he can win a coveted Super Bowl ring.

The Los Angeles Chargers are winless. Mack seems to be nearing the end of his career but could get a boost in a winning situation. Mack could add more depth to the 49ers' defensive front. He has three sacks and 11 tackles this season. His snaps might be limited, but he could be a force rushing the passer and even spell Nick Bosa when he returns.

TRADE DEADLINE: From the Bills to 49ers, 1 NFL trade target for each division leader

Will McDonald IV could be the power punch on the edge that the 49ers are looking for. He plays a complementary role in New York, but he can be effective when he sees snaps. McDonald is a 6-foot-4, 245-pound pass rusher who has 23.5 career sacks and four forced fumbles in 54 games.

That kind of addition could give San Francisco another weapon on the line as they look to fill the void left by injuries. McDonald possesses a skill set that would complement the 49ers' existing defensive line with a healthy Keion White, Gracen Halton, Osa Odighizuwa, Romello Height and Mykel Williams.

So far in 2026, he has three sacks and seven total tackles.

The 49ers already knocked on the Colts' door to make a trade; why not do it again? San Francisco needs wide receivers and Indianapolis is apparently undergoing a fire sale. The 49ers would do themselves a favor by going after Josh Downs. The 25-year-old wide receiver has 30 catches, 330 receiving yards and one touchdown this season.

He would give the Niners another fresh look at wideout lining up predominantly in the slot. He's shifty in his cuts and is another QB-friendly target. He would be a solid addition for San Francisco as they ease back Mike Evans and Deebo Samuel from injury. The 49ers could await for their rookie De'Zhaun Stribling to return but in the meantime, a trade would mean they don't have to rush him back, or put additional pressure on him to perform.

#2795. Jerry Jones wants to see more targets for George Pickens

The 3-5 Cowboys have plenty of problems. During the first of his two weekly radio spots on 105.3 The Fan in Dallas, owner and G.M. Jerry Jones mentioned one specific thing that he’d like to see change.

Regarding receiver George Pickens, Jones said this, via Jon Machota of The Athletic: “He needs the ball coming his way more.”

Through eight games, Pickens has 62 targets. In Week 8 against the Cardinals, Pickens had the ball thrown in his direction only four times. Against the Eagles in Week 7, Pickens had six targets.

He has 39 catches for 533 yards and two touchdowns in 2026. That projects to 83 catches for 1,132 yards and 8.5 touchdowns.

In 2025, Pickens finished with 93 receptions, 1,429 yards, and nine scores.

Hovering over the situation is the decision the Cowboys will have to make after the season. He’s making $27.298 million under the franchise tag; they could tag him again next year, at $32.758 million. Which is still nearly $10 million behind the top of the receiver market.

There’s another option, now that the Cowboys are fading in the race for the NFC East title. They could make Pickens available in trade.

Would a contender offer a second-round pick for him? The Chiefs are currently desperate for help at receiver; they’re interested (we’re told) in Jaguars receiver Brian Thomas Jr. and Colts receiver Josh Downs. If the Chiefs are in on Pickens, other teams in the AFC (like the Bills) should be thinking about it, too.

If the Cowboys were to decide to make a move, they could stockpile a pick or two — and save $15.17 million in cash and cap space. For now, who knows? The Cowboys seem to be deluding themselves into thinking they’re still contenders.

Unless that’s just part of the annual effort to keep their fans coughing up cash for tickets and merch. Which continues to work very well, relative to the on-field production.

#2794. Rockets top NBA power rankings for the week

The Houston Rockets are off to a red-hot start, leading the NBA with a 7-1 record, as well as holding wins over the Boston Celtics, Oklahoma City Thunder, and San Antonio Spurs. The team has looked like the same hard-nosed defensive team we have grown used to, but this time with some idea of an offensive plan. Granted, it's early in the season, but the Rockets look good right now, and it seems some others might agree. The Athletic released it's new series of NBA power rankings, and low and behold, who sits atop at the moment but your Houston Rockets. Here's what they had to say about our boys:

Last ranking: FourthIn the last week: W vs ATL, W at MIL, L at DAL, W vs OKCOffensive rating: 121.2 (first place)Defensive rating: 108.9 (ninth place)

Starters: Fred VanVleet, Amen Thompson, Kevin Durant, Jabari Smith Jr., Alperen ŞengünKey reserves: Marcus Smart, Tari Eason, Reed Sheppard, Clint Capela, Bogdan Bogdanović

Houston's improved depth hasn't quite made the defense special, which makes sense. The Rockets don't have great rim protection, they have a small guard in the starting lineup and their best player is 38. The Rockets tried to get away with lineups without centers in Dallas with Şengün nursing a back injury, and it's one reason they aren't undefeated anymore. Right behind Houston are the 5-1 Detroit Pistons and the 6-1 Philadelphia LeBrons. I think the Rockets are better than either of those teams, but time will tell. For now, enjoy the the early-season success.

#2793. Another Gauthier hat trick, Ducks hot power play payback Canucks

VANCOUVER, B.C. – Last Tuesday in Anaheim, it was an "unacceptable," "terrible" and "careless" collapse to the last-in-the-league Vancouver Canucks.

This Tuesday in Vancouver, there was another offensive surge, and despite a push from the hometown Canucks, the Anaheim Ducks rode history to a payback victory.

Cutter Gauthier became the first player in Ducks history to net hat tricks in back to back games, as Anaheim's historically hot power play unit buoyed a 5-4 road victory at Rogers Arena on Tuesday.

Anaheim (7-7-1, 15 points) has scored 29 goals in the last five games and is 10 for 12 on the power play in the last three games. Ducks are the 10th team in NHL history to score three power play goals in three straight games and the first since the then-Mighty Ducks did it in March 2006.

Anaheim's 10 power play goals in three games is the most in the NHL since the 2008-09 Minnesota Wild, and Gauthier is the first player in NHL history with multiple power play goals in three straight games.

Nov 3, 2026; Vancouver, British Columbia, CAN; Anaheim Ducks forward Cutter Gauthier (61) celebrates after scoring a goal against the Vancouver Canucks in the first period at Rogers Arena.

Mikael Granlund opened the power play scoring, and after Vancouver evened on its own power play, Cutter Gauthier scored his first of two with the extra man. Leo Carlsson scored a shorthanded snipe for a 3-1 lead, and Gauthier's second gave the Ducks a 4-2 lead into the third period.

Gauthier ripped his third goal less than five minutes into the third period for his second consecutive hat trick and a 5-2 lead.

Gauthier has scored goals in just four games this season, but each one has been a multi-goal night. He is the only the second player in Ducks history with three straight multi-goal games, joining Paul Kariya in 2000-01.

Gauthier has eight goals in those last three games and is the first player age 22 or younger to do so since Patrik Laine in 1998 and just the second since Teemu Selanne in 1993.

Beckett Sennecke assisted on all three Gauthier goals for his first career four-assist game. He is the fourth Duck in the last decade to register a four-assist game joining Ryan Getzlaf, Corey Perry and Nikita Nesterenko.

Liam Ohgren scored the Canucks even-up power play goal, 1-1, and Linus Karlsson scored off a turnover to cut the Ducks lead to 3-2. Jake Debrusk scored on the power play and again at five-on-five in the third period to bring Vancouver back within one goal, 5-4.

Laurent Brossoit stopped 19 of 23 shots in his second straight start as a Duck since being called up from AHL San Diego. Kevin Lankinen made 17 saves for the Canucks.

Anaheim continues its three-game Western Canada road trip with the first half of a back-to-back in Edmonton on Thursday. The Ducks then play in Calgary on Saturday before returning home to host Pittsburgh next Sunday.

Anaheim will debut its Hometown Remix jersey on Sunday against the Penguins. The sweaters for that league-wide alternate uniform initiative were unveiled yesterday morning and resulted in the Ducks' most single-day jersey sales at the Anaheim team store since the inaugural Mighty Ducks jersey in 1993.

Nico Myatovic made his NHL debut on Tuesday. The Prince George, British Columbia native is the second player of Anaheim's 2023 draft class to play in the NHL following Leo Carlsson.

Myatovic was called up from AHL San Diego to fill in for AJ Greer, who was placed on injured reserve yesterday with an upper-body injury. Greer took a blindside hit late against Ottawa on Sunday and did not return.

Jackson LaCombe earned an assist for the eighth straight game tying Francois Beauchemin for the second-longest such streak by a Ducks defenseman. Scott Neidermayer sits just one game ahead with a franchise-best nine-game streak.

Beckett Sennecke is the youngest player in Ducks history to record a four-assist game at 20 years, 279 days old. He beats out Ryan Getzlaf (20 years, 318 days) and Corey Perry (23 years, 168 days).

#2789. Why Luka Doncic is among great scorers — of all time

It's easy to overlook what Luka Dončić is doing.

He has been putting up video game numbers for so long that we're almost inured to them in the same way our olfactory senses stop responding to a constant smell.

But Giannis Antetokounmpo slapped us out of our slumber following Dončić's fourth 40-point performance in five days in the Lakers' 138-117 win over the Heat on Monday.

"Obviously he's one of the best scorers to ever play this game, if not the best," Antetokounmpo said. "Not right now. Of all time. I feel like some people are scared to say that. But it's got to be out there."

Dončić has led the league in scoring twice and is on pace to do so a third time. He's averaging a blistering 38.3 points per game, 8.3 points more than second-place Stephen Curry. Here are the points Dončić has put up in the last five games: 42, 45, 37, 41, 52.

Yet somehow we've never thought of Dončić as an all-time scorer.

While Curry is the consensus greatest shooter ever, different players have been thrown into the conversation for all-time greatest scorer, including Michael Jordan, Wilt Chamberlain and even Kevin Durant, the latter of whom is a beacon of consistency but isn't going to regularly explode for 50-plus points.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers dribbles during a game against the Miami Heat at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

He has a career scoring average of 29.32 points, trailing only Jordan (30.12) and Chamberlain (30.07).

And Dončić is only 27 years old. He's still in his prime. He's only getting better.

Before JJ Redick became the Lakers' coach in June 2024, he said Dončić "has the best brakes in the fu–king world" on his former podcast, "The Old Man & The Three."

"What he does is there's a constant change of pace," Redick said. "He's on-balance. He's trying to get you off-balance. Once you're off-balance, he's still on-balance, he changes his pace."

In layman's terms: Dončić is impossible to guard.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic (77) drives past Miami Heat guard Klay Thompson in the second half of an NBA game in Los Angeles, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

Heat coach Erik Spoelstra knows that all too well. When asked about defending Dončić ahead of Monday's game, he pretty much intimated that it's a fool's errand.

"It's not going to be two defenders on him or even three," Spoelstra said. "You're pretty much going to go through your entire roster."

Dončić is an offensive wizard. He's a savant. He makes scoring look easy. And it's time for him to be considered among the all-time greats.

Against the Heat on Monday, Dončić had 24 points at halftime, becoming the first Laker to score at least 20 points in the first half in five straight games since Kobe Bryant in 2007.

He's averaging a historical 1.07 points per minute. The only other player who surpassed 1.0 was Chamberlain, who averaged 1.04 points per minute in 1961-62.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers drives to the basket against the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

And Dončić is scoring at an extremely efficient rate, averaging career highs in field-goal percentage (49.7) and 3-point percentage (42.7). He's also third in the league in assists with eight per game. Heck, he's even playing defense.

He's having one of the best starts in NBA history, becoming the third player to score at least 225 points through his first six games, alongside Jordan (1985-86) and Chamberlain, who did it twice in 1961-62 and 1962-63.

Jordan led the league in scoring 10 seasons and is widely considered the greatest player of all time. Chamberlain averaged 50.4 points per game during the 1961-62 season and is the only player to score 100 points in a game.

The greatest scorer of all time is not an easy debate to enter. But in the modern NBA, does anyone compare to Dončić? And historically, Dončić is already etching his name into the record books.
